The relative lattice path operad
Alexandre Quesney

Abstract
We construct a set-theoretic coloured operad ℛ ​ ℒ \mathcal{RL} that may be thought of as a combinatorial model for the Swiss Cheese operad. This is the relative (or Swiss Cheese) version of the lattice path operad constructed by Batanin and Berger. By adapting their condensation process we obtain a topological (resp. chain) operad that we show to be weakly equivalent to the topological (resp. chain) Swiss Cheese operad.



1
Introduction

The Swiss Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}
is a 2-coloured topological operad that mixes, in its
m
-dimensional part
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
, the
m
-dimensional and the
(m-1)
-dimensional parts of the little cubes operad
\mathcal{C}
.
It was introduced by A. Voronov
[
Vor98
]
as a natural way to define actions of
C_{*}(\mathcal{C}_{m})
-algebras on
C_{*}(\mathcal{C}_{m-1})
-algebras and has been used by M. Kontsevich
[
Kon99
]
in deformation quantization. As announced in
[
HLS13
]
, the Swiss Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
also recognizes the pair (
m
-fold loop space,
m
-fold relative loop space).
The goal of this paper is to provide a convenient combinatorial model for (the operad of chains of)
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
,
m\geq 1
.

In
[
BB09
]
, Batanin and Berger introduce the notion of
condensation
of a coloured operad.
By applying this condensation to the
lattice path operad

\mathcal{L}
they obtain a model for the (operad of chains of the) little cubes operad.

We introduce the
relative lattice path operad

\mathcal{RL}
. It is a coloured operad in the category of sets that has two types of colours (closed and open). Taking a cosimplicial object
\delta:\bigtriangleup\to\textbf{C}
in a cocomplete closed monoidal symmetric category
C
,
we adapt Batanin-Berger’s method to obtain a functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{RL}\text{-algebra}\longrightarrow\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}}(\delta)\text{-algebra}

that sends algebras over
\mathcal{RL}
into algebras over the
condensation operad
of
\mathcal{RL}
, that is, the
SC type
operad

\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}}(\delta)
.

The operad
\mathcal{RL}
has two filtrations by suboperads
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
,
{m\geq 1}
; they differ one from each other by their open/closed interacting part.

We are interested by two choices for
\delta
:

\delta_{Top}:\triangle
\textbf{Set}^{\bigtriangleup^{\text{op}}}
Top
\delta_{yon}
|-|

and

\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}:\triangle
\textbf{Set}^{\bigtriangleup^{\text{op}}}
\textbf{Ch}(\mathbb{Z})
,
\delta_{yon}
C_{*}(-;\mathbb{Z})

where
\delta_{yon}([n])=\Hom_{\triangle}(-,[n])
is the Yoneda functor.
In this manner, the condensation of
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
leads to a topological operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\text{Top}})
and to a chain operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
, and similarly for
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
.

In the topological case, both the SC type operads
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\text{Top}})
and
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{\text{Top}})
naturally act on the pair (
m
-fold loop space,
m
-fold relative loop space).

In order to rely our operads to the Swiss Cheese operad, we use Berger’s method of cellular decompositions.
The Swiss Cheese operad that we consider is denoted by
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
,
m\geq 1
, and is the augmented (cubical) version of Voronov’s Swiss Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}_{m}^{\text{vor}}
defined in
[
Vor98
]
.
We construct a cellular decomposition of
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
that generalizes
Berger’s cell decomposition of the little
m
-cubes operad
[
Ber97
]
.
The latter is indexed by the
extended complete graph operad

\mathcal{K}_{m}
.
In contrast to the non-relative case, there are two ways for indexing the cells of
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
. This
naturally leads us to consider two different indexing operads
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
and
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
that may be thought of as the relative versions of
\mathcal{K}_{m}
.
One obtains:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
m\geq 1
. Any topological
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
-cellular operad (resp.
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
-cellular operad) is weakly equivalent to the Swiss Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
.

The operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta)
benefits of a decomposition by “cells” that are indexed by the poset operad
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
. This is obtained by means of a map
c_{tot}:\mathcal{RL}_{m}\to\mathcal{RK}_{m}
satisfying
c_{tot}(x\circ_{i}y)\leq c_{tot}(x)\circ_{i}c_{tot}(y)
. From this (and similar considerations for
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
), one obtains:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
m\geq 1
. The operads
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\text{Top}})
and
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{\text{Top}})

are weakly equivalent to the topological Swiss-Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
.
The operads
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
and
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
are weakly equivalent to the chain Swiss-Cheese operad
C_{*}(\mathcal{SC}_{m})
.

Note that, for each
m\geq 1
, the operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
(resp.
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
) admits a weakly equivalent suboperad
\mathcal{RS}_{m}
(resp
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}
).
These two operads
\mathcal{RS}_{m}
and
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}
are relative versions of the
surjection operad

\mathcal{S}_{m}
studied by McClure-Smith
[
MS02
,
MS03
]
and Berger-Fresse
[
BF04
]
.

With regards to future applications, we pay close attention to the operads
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
.
They benefit of a description by planar rooted trees with different types of vertices.
The operad
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
encodes pairs
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})
where
\mathcal{M}
is a multiplicative non-symmetric operad and
\mathcal{Z}
is bimodule over
\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{A}s
(left action of
\mathcal{M}
, right action of
\mathcal{A}s
) where
\mathcal{A}s
is the operad of associative algebras; such a pair is naturally endowed with a morphism of bimodules
\iota:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{Z}
.
The operad
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
encodes almost similar pairs
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
where, instead,
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
and
\iota:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
are in the category of bimodules over
\mathcal{A}s-\mathcal{M}
.

Outline of the paper.

Section
2
is first devoted in setting our conventions and notations on non-symmetric, symmetric, coloured and SC type operads. In particular, we spend time on modules and weak modules over a non-symmetric operad; this will be used in the last section
6
. Afterwards, we define the (symmetric)
coloured SC-operads
and we explain how we
condense
them to obtain SC type operads.

In section
3
we consider the (cubical) Swiss Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}
. For each integer
m\geq 1
, we construct two cellular decompositions of
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
, one indexed by
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
the other one indexed by
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
.

Section
4
concerns the relative lattice path operad
\mathcal{RL}
which is a coloured SC-operad.
By using the condensation process from Section
2
one obtains an SC type operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}}(\delta)
.
We use results of Section
3
to prove Theorem
1.2
. We end the section by exhibiting a few examples of representations of
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
. In particular, we show that the operads
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\text{Top}})
and
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{\text{Top}})
act on the pair (
m
-fold loop space,
m
-fold relative loop space).

In Section
5
we exhibit the sub-operads
\mathcal{RS}_{m}
and
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}
and show that the inclusions
\mathcal{RS}_{m}\hookrightarrow\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
and
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}\hookrightarrow\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
are weak equivalences.

In Section
6
we focus on the operads
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
and their representations.
We describe
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
in terms of planar rooted trees. This provides a convenient language for describing the representations of
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Eduardo Hoefel for his constant support.

2
Preliminaries

All along the paper
\textbf{C}=(C,\otimes,1_{\textbf{C}})
denotes a cocomplete closed monoidal symmetric category.
In particular,
C
is endowed with an object
0\in\textbf{C}
such that
0\otimes X=0=X\otimes 0
for all
X\in\textbf{C}
.

For two
C
-categories (
i.e.
enriched over
C
)
A
and
B
, we denote by
\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{B}
the category with the pairs
(a,b)
for
a\in\textbf{A}
and
b\in\textbf{B}
as objects and

\Hom_{\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{B}}((a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})):=\Hom_{\textbf{A}}(a,a^{\prime})\otimes\Hom_{\textbf{B}}(b,b^{\prime})

as hom-objects, where the tensor on the right hand side is the tensor of
C
.

2.1
Non-symmetric operads and modules

By a
non-symmetric (non-
\Sigma
) operad

\cal{M}
we mean a collection
\{\mathcal{M}(n)\}_{n\geq 0}
of objects of
C
together with a unit
\eta:1_{\textbf{C}}\to\mathcal{M}(1)
and partial composition maps
\circ_{i}:\mathcal{M}(m)\otimes\mathcal{M}(n)\to\mathcal{M}(m+n-1)
, for
1\leq i\leq m
,
that satisfy associativity and unit conditions (see
[
MSS02
, Definition 1.14]
).
One denotes by
\mathcal{A}s
the non-
\Sigma
operad of associative algebras in
C
given by
\mathcal{A}s(n)=1_{\textbf{C}}
for
n\geq 0
.
A non-
\Sigma
operad
\mathcal{M}
is called
multiplicative
if there is a morphism of operads
\alpha:\mathcal{A}s\to\mathcal{M}
.
A morphism of multiplicative operads
\alpha:\mathcal{A}s\to\mathcal{M}
and
\alpha^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}s\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a morphism of operads
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
such that
f\circ\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
.

In what follows, one recalls the notions of left, right, bi module over a non-
\Sigma
operad.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
be two non-
\Sigma
operads and let
\mathcal{Z}=\{\mathcal{Z}(m)\}_{m\geq 0}
be a collection of objects of
C
.
Consider the following morphisms:

\rho_{i}:\mathcal{Z}(m)\otimes\mathcal{N}(k)\to\mathcal{Z}(k+m-1)\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ for }1\leq i\leq m\penalty\ \text{(right action)}

\lambda:\mathcal{M}(k)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m_{k})\to\mathcal{Z}(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k})\penalty\ \text{(left action).}

Consider the following relations.

1.

Unit condition for the left action:

\mathcal{Z}(m)=1_{\textbf{C}}\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m)
\mathcal{M}(1)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m)
\mathcal{Z}(m)
\eta\otimes id
\lambda

is the identity.

2.

Associativity of the left action:
all the diagrams of the following form commute

\mathcal{M}(k)\otimes\mathcal{M}(l)\otimes\bigotimes_{1\leq p\leq k+l-1}\mathcal{Z}(m_{p})
\mathcal{M}(k)\bigotimes_{1\leq p<i}\mathcal{Z}(m_{p})\otimes\mathcal{Z}(x)\otimes\bigotimes_{i+l\leq p\leq k+l-1}\mathcal{Z}(m_{p})
\mathcal{M}(k+l-1)\otimes\bigotimes_{1\leq p\leq k+l-1}\mathcal{Z}(m_{p})
\mathcal{Z}(m_{1}+...+m_{k+l-1})
(id\otimes\lambda\otimes id)\circ\tau_{2,(3,i+1)}
(-\circ_{i}-)\otimes id
\lambda
\lambda

where
x:=m_{i}+...+m_{i+l-1}
, for
1\leq i\leq k
, and
\tau_{2,(3,i+1)}
denotes the permutation of the second factor with the
i-1
factors that follow.

3.

Unity condition of right action:

\mathcal{Z}(m)=\mathcal{Z}(m)\otimes 1_{\textbf{C}}
\mathcal{Z}(m)\otimes\mathcal{N}(1)
\mathcal{Z}(m)
\eta\otimes id
\rho_{i}

is the identity.

4.

Associativity and commutativity of right action on different inputs: all the diagrams of the following form commute

\mathcal{Z}(m)\otimes\mathcal{N}(k)\otimes\mathcal{N}(l)
\mathcal{Z}(m+k-1)\otimes\mathcal{N}(l)
\mathcal{Z}(m)\otimes\mathcal{N}(k+l-1)
\mathcal{Z}(m+k+l-2)
.
\rho_{t}\otimes id
id\otimes(-\circ_{i}-)
\rho_{t+i-1}
\rho_{t}

\mathcal{Z}(m)\otimes\mathcal{N}(k)\otimes\mathcal{N}(l)
\mathcal{Z}(m+k-1)\otimes\mathcal{N}(l)
\mathcal{Z}(m+l-1)\otimes\mathcal{N}(k)
\mathcal{Z}(m+k+l-2)
.
\rho_{t}\otimes id
\rho_{t}\circ\tau_{2,3}
\rho_{t+i-1}
\rho_{i}

5.

Associativity of right and left actions:
all the diagrams of the following form commute

\mathcal{M}(k)\otimes\bigotimes_{1\leq p\leq k}\mathcal{Z}(m_{p})\otimes\mathcal{N}(l)
\mathcal{M}(k)\bigotimes_{1\leq p<s}\mathcal{Z}(m_{p})\otimes\mathcal{Z}(x)\otimes\bigotimes_{s<p\leq k}\mathcal{Z}(m_{p})
\mathcal{Z}(m_{1}+...+m_{k})\otimes\mathcal{N}(l)
\mathcal{Z}(m_{1}+...+m_{k}+l-1)
,
(id\otimes id^{\otimes s-1}\otimes\rho_{i}\otimes id^{\otimes k-s})\circ\tau_{(s+2,k+1),k+2}
\lambda\otimes id
\lambda
\rho_{i+m_{1}+...+m_{s-1}-s+1}

where
x=m_{s}+l-1
.

We establish the following terminology:

•

(\mathcal{Z},\lambda)
is called a
left module
over
\cal{M}
if
\lambda
satisfies (1) and (2).

•

(\mathcal{Z},\rho)
is called a
right module
over
\cal{N}
if the
\rho_{i}
satisfy (3) and (4).

•

(\mathcal{Z},\lambda,\rho)
is called a
bimodule
over
\cal{M}-\mathcal{N}
if
\lambda
and
\rho_{i}
satisfy (1)-(5).

Example 2.2
.

A non-
\Sigma
operad
\mathcal{M}
is canonically a bimodule over itself (
i.e.
over
\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{M}
).

Given two non-
\Sigma
operads
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
, the category of bimodules over
\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{N}
is denoted by
\textbf{BiMod}_{\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{N}}
; the morphisms are morphisms that commute with each of the actions maps.
A bimodule over
\cal{M}-\mathcal{M}
is simply called a bimodule over
\cal{M}
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
be a left (resp. right, bi) module over
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
A morphism of non-
\Sigma
operads
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
endows
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
with a structure of left (resp. right, bi) module over
\mathcal{M}
by pulling back the actions maps along
f
; we denote the resulting left (resp. right, bi) module over
\mathcal{M}
by
f^{*}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
. Explicitly,
\lambda^{f^{*}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}=\lambda^{\prime}\circ(f\otimes id^{\otimes k})
and
\rho_{i}^{f^{*}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}=\rho_{i}^{\prime}\circ(id\otimes f)
.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{Z}
is a left (resp. right, bi) module over
\mathcal{M}
, then a morphism
g:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is said
f
-equivariant
if it is a morphism of left (resp. right, bi) modules over
\mathcal{M}
,
g:\mathcal{Z}\to f^{*}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
.

Example 2.4
.

A left module over a multiplicative operad is, by pullback, a left module over
\mathcal{A}s
.

In
[
Tur10
, Definition 4.1]
, the notion of
weak
bimodule over a non-
\Sigma
operad
\mathcal{M}
(in
Top
) is introduced. In our framework, it refers to a right module over
\mathcal{M}
together with a
weak
left action

\lambda^{w}_{i}:\mathcal{M}(k)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m)\to\mathcal{Z}(k+m-1)\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ for }1\leq i\leq k,

that satisfies natural associativity and unit conditions
i.e.
(1) above and (W1)-(W3) below.

W1.

Associativity of the weak left action:
all the diagrams of the following form commute

\scalebox{.9}{

W2.

Associativity of right and left actions:
all the diagrams of the following form commute

\scalebox{.9}{

W3.

Compatibility between the actions and the operad composition:
all the diagrams of the following form commute

\mathcal{M}(k)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m)\otimes\mathcal{M}(l)
\mathcal{M}(k+l-1)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m)
\mathcal{Z}(k+m-1)\otimes\mathcal{M}(l)
\mathcal{Z}(k+m+l-2)
((-\circ_{t}-)\otimes id)\circ\tau_{2,3}
\lambda^{w}_{i}\otimes id
\lambda^{w}_{i+l-1}
\rho_{t}

\mathcal{M}(k)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m)\otimes\mathcal{M}(l)
\mathcal{M}(k+l-1)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m)
\mathcal{Z}(k+m-1)\otimes\mathcal{M}(l)
\mathcal{Z}(k+m+l-2)
,
((-\circ_{t}-)\otimes id)\circ\tau_{2,3}
\lambda^{w}_{i}\otimes id
\lambda^{w}_{i}
\rho_{t+m-1}

for
1\leq t<i\leq k
in the left-sided diagram and
1\leq i<t\leq k
in the right-sided diagram.

Example 2.5
.

A non-
\Sigma
operad
\mathcal{M}
is a weak bimodule over itself.

Example 2.6
.

Consider a bimodule
\mathcal{Z}
over
\mathcal{M}
and suppose it comes together with a bimodule map
\iota:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{Z}
; recall that
\eta:1_{\textbf{C}}\to\mathcal{M}(1)
denotes the unit.
Pre-composing the left action
\lambda
of
\mathcal{Z}
by
\iota\eta
at all but one input provides maps
\lambda^{w}_{i}:=\lambda\circ(id\otimes(\iota\eta)^{\otimes i-1}\otimes id\otimes(\iota\eta)^{\otimes k-i}):\mathcal{M}(k)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m)\to\mathcal{Z}(k+m-1)
that endow
\mathcal{Z}
with a weak bimodule structure over
\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, for this structure,
\iota
is a morphism of weak bimodules.

As observed in
[
Tur10
, Lemma 4.2]
, one of the interesting features of the
weak
bimodules is the following.

Lemma 2.7
.

The structure of a cosimplicial object is equivalent to the structure of a weak bimodule over
\mathcal{A}s
.

2.2
Coloured operads

Coloured (symmetric) operads are also known as
small symmetric multicategories
, see
[
Lei04
, Definition 2.2.21]
. For our purposes one uses left actions for the symmetric groups
\Sigma_{n}
, so that a coloured operad with set of colours
Col
(or a
Col
-coloured operad) consists of:

•

for each
k\geq 0
and each
(k+1)
-tuple
(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
of colours
e_{i},e\in Col
, an object

\mathcal{O}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
in
C
;

•

for each colour
e
, a unit
1_{\textbf{C}}\to\mathcal{O}(e;e)
;

•

for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e)
and
(f_{1},\dots,f_{l};e_{i})
, substitution maps

\circ_{i}:\mathcal{O}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e)\otimes\mathcal{O}(f_{1},\dots,f_{l};e_{i})\to\mathcal{O}(e_{1},\dots,e_{i-1},f_{1},\dots,f_{l},e_{i+1},\dots,e_{k};e)

•

for each
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
, a map
\sigma_{*}:\mathcal{O}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e)\to\mathcal{O}(e_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma^{-1}(k)};e)
.

Substitution maps are required to satisfy the natural unit, associativity and equivariance axioms.

Example 2.8
.

A
symmetric
operad
\mathcal{P}
is a
1
-coloured operad with
\mathcal{P}(k)=\mathcal{P}(\underbrace{*,...,*}_{k};*)
.

For a coloured operad
\mathcal{O}
, the category of its unary operations is called its
underlying category
and is denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{u}
: the objects of
\mathcal{O}_{u}
are the colours; the morphisms are
\mathcal{O}_{u}(e,f):=\mathcal{O}(e;f)
for
e,f\in{Col}
.
The operadic structure of
\mathcal{O}
is encoded as functors

\mathcal{O}(\underbrace{-,\cdots,-}_{k};-):(\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\text{op}})^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{u}\to\textbf{C},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ k\geq 0.

Recall that an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
X
is a family
\{X(e)\}_{e\in Col}
of objects
X(e)\in\textbf{C}
equipped with
morphisms

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)\otimes X(e_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes X(e_{k})\to X(e),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ e_{1},...,e_{k},e\in Col

subject to the natural unit, associative and equivariance axioms.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
C
be a
monoidal model category
; see
[
Hov99
]
.
A morphism of
Col
-coloured operads
\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is a
weak equivalence
if and only if each of its components
\mathcal{O}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k};n)\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k};n)
is a weak equivalence.
Two
Col
-coloured operads
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
are said
weakly equivalent
if there is a zig-zag of weak equivalences of
Col
-coloured operads
\mathcal{O}\leftarrow\cdot\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.10
.

The weak equivalence as described above is part of the model category structure on the category of
Col
-coloured operads as established in
[
BM07
, Section 3]
whenever
C
satisfies properties of
loc. cit., Theorem 2.1
.

2.3
SC-operads and coloured SC-operads

The SC type operads (see
[
Bat07
]
) are a special type of
2
-coloured operads whose structure mimics that of the Swiss-Cheese operad introduced in
[
Vor98
]
. Explicitly, by an
SC type operad
(or SC-operad), one means a
\{\mathfrak{cl},\mathfrak{op}\}
-coloured operad
\mathcal{O}
such that
\mathcal{O}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{cl})=0
if there exists a
1\leq i\leq n
such that
c_{i}=\mathfrak{op}
.
The colour
\mathfrak{cl}
is called the
closed
colour; the colour
\mathfrak{op}
is called the
open
colour.

Let us define the coloured version of the SC type operads.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
Col
be a set (of colours).
A
coloured SC-operad
is a
Col
-coloured operad
\mathcal{O}
that satisfies the following hypotheses.

H1.

Col={Col}_{\mathfrak{cl}}\sqcup{Col}_{\mathfrak{op}}
.

H2.

The collection of the
\mathcal{O}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e)
for
e_{i},e\in{Col}_{\mathfrak{cl}}
and
k\geq 0
forms a sub-operad of
\mathcal{O}
.

H3.

The collection of the
\mathcal{O}(e_{1},\dots,e_{j};e)
for
e_{i},e\in{Col}_{\mathfrak{op}}
and
j\geq 0
forms a sub-operad of
\mathcal{O}
.

H4.

\mathcal{O}(e_{1},\dots,e_{j};e)=0\in\textbf{C}
for any
e\in Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}
if there exists
1\leq i\leq j
, such that
e_{i}\in{Col}_{\mathfrak{op}}
, where
j\geq 1
.

The sub-operad in H2 is called the
closed part of
\mathcal{O}
; the sub-operad in H3 is called the
open part of
\mathcal{O}
. For
c\in\{\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op}\}
, a colour of
{Col}_{c}
is called
colour of type

c
or
c
colour
.

Example 2.12
.

An SC type operad is a
Col
-coloured SC-operad with
Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}=\{\mathfrak{cl}\}
and
Col_{\mathfrak{op}}=\{\mathfrak{op}\}
.

The underlying category
\mathcal{O}_{u}
of a coloured SC-operad contains two particular categories:

•

\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}
is the sub-category of
\mathcal{O}_{u}
with objects the colours in
{Col}_{\mathfrak{cl}}
and morphisms the
\mathcal{O}_{u}(e,f)
for
e,f\in{Col}_{\mathfrak{cl}}
;

•

\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{op}}
is the sub-category of
\mathcal{O}_{u}
with objects the colours in
{Col}_{\mathfrak{op}}
and morphisms the
\mathcal{O}_{u}(e,f)
for
e,f\in{Col}_{\mathfrak{op}}
.

Note also that, from the hypothesis on
\mathcal{O}
, an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
X
can be seen as a pair
(X_{\mathfrak{cl}},X_{\mathfrak{op}})
where
X_{\mathfrak{cl}}
is the sub-family
\{X_{\mathfrak{cl}}(e)\}_{e\in Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}}
and
X_{\mathfrak{op}}
is the sub-family
\{X_{\mathfrak{op}}(e)\}_{e\in Col_{\mathfrak{op}}}
.

2.4
SC functor-operads

Here one defines the SC analogues to the functor-operads and their algebras.
The notion of functor-operad is introduced in
[
MS04
]
and generalizes the notion of operad (see also
[
BB09
]
).

Let us fix
A
and
B
be two
C
-categories.

For
k\geq 0
, for a collection of
C
-functors

\{\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}:\textbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\textbf{A}_{k}\to\textbf{A}_{k+1}\}_{\textbf{A}_{i}\in\{\textbf{A},\textbf{B}\}}

and for
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
,
we denote by
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}^{\sigma}:\textbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\textbf{A}_{k}\to\textbf{A}_{k+1}
the functor

\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}^{\sigma}(X_{1},...,X_{k})=\xi_{\textbf{A}_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},...,\textbf{A}_{\sigma^{-1}(k)};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}(X_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},...,X_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}).

A collection of
C
-functors

\{\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}:\textbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\textbf{A}_{k}\to\textbf{A}_{k+1}\}_{\textbf{A}_{i}\in\{\textbf{A},\textbf{B}\}}

is called
twisted symmetric
if there exist
C
-natural transformations

\phi_{\sigma,\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}:\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\to\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}^{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
, such that

\phi_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2},\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}=(\phi_{\sigma_{1},\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}})^{\sigma_{2}}\phi_{\sigma_{2},\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}

and such that
\phi_{id,\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
is the identity transformation where
id
denotes the neutral element of
\Sigma_{k}
.

Definition 2.13
.

An
SC functor-operad

\xi=\{\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\}_{k,\textbf{A}_{i}}
over
(\textbf{A},\textbf{B})
is the
data, for each
k\geq 0
, of a twisted symmetric collection

\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}:\textbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\textbf{A}_{k}\to\textbf{A}_{k+1}

indexed by the
(k+1)
-tuples
(\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1})
of categories in
\{\textbf{A},\textbf{B}\}
such that
\textbf{A}_{k+1}=\textbf{B}
whenever there exists
1\leq i\leq k
such that
\textbf{A}_{i}=\textbf{B}
.
Such a collection is required to be endowed with natural transformations

\mu_{[\textbf{A}]_{1,i_{1}},...,[\textbf{A}]_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}:\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\circ(\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1,1},...,\textbf{A}_{1,i_{1}};\textbf{A}_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\xi_{\textbf{A}_{k,1},...,\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k}})\\
\to\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1,1},...,\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ for }i_{1},...,i_{k}\geq 0,

where
[\textbf{A}]_{a,b}=(\textbf{A}_{a,1},...,\textbf{A}_{a,b};\textbf{A}_{a})
.
These natural transformations have to satisfy the following three conditions.

1.

For
\textbf{A}_{0}\in\{\textbf{A},\textbf{B}\}
, the functor
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{0};\textbf{A}_{0}}
is the identity and
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{k+1};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\circ\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}=\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}=\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\circ(\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1};\textbf{A}_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\xi_{\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k}})
where the equalities are obtained via
\mu_{(\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1});\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
and
\mu_{(\textbf{A}_{1};\textbf{A}_{1}),...,(\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k});\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
respectively.

2.

The natural transformations
\mu_{[\textbf{A}]_{1,i_{1}},...,[\textbf{A}]_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
are associative.

3.

All diagrams of the following forms commute:
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\circ(\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1,1},...,\textbf{A}_{1,i_{1}};\textbf{A}_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\xi_{\textbf{A}_{k,1},...,\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k}})
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1,1},...,\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}^{\sigma}\circ(\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1,1},...,\textbf{A}_{1,i_{1}};\textbf{A}_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\xi_{\textbf{A}_{k,1},...,\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k}}^{\sigma_{k}})
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1,1},...,\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}^{\sigma(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{k+j})}
.
\phi_{\sigma}\circ(\phi_{\sigma_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\phi_{\sigma_{k+j}})
\mu_{[\textbf{A}]_{1,i_{1}},...,[\textbf{A}]_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
\phi_{\sigma(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k+j})}
\mu_{[\textbf{A}]_{1,i_{1}},...,[\textbf{A}]_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\xi=\{\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\}_{k\geq 0}
be an SC functor-operad over
(\textbf{A},\textbf{B})
.
A
\xi
-algebra

X
is a pair
(X_{\textbf{A}},X_{\textbf{B}})\in\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{B}
equipped with morphisms in
\textbf{A}_{k+1}
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}:\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}(X_{\textbf{A}_{1}},...,X_{\textbf{A}_{k}})

\displaystyle\to X_{\textbf{A}_{k+1}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ k\geq 0,

subject to the following conditions.

1.

\alpha_{\textbf{A}_{1}}=1_{X_{\textbf{A}_{1}}}
;

2.

\alpha_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\circ\phi_{\sigma}=\alpha_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
, for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
;

3.

all the diagrams of the following form commute

\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}\circ(\xi_{[\textbf{A}]_{1,i_{1}}}(X_{1,i_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes\xi_{[\textbf{A}]_{k,i_{k}}}(X_{k,i_{k}}))
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1,1},...,\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}(X_{\textbf{A}_{1,1}},...,X_{\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}}})
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}(X_{\textbf{A}_{1}},...,X_{\textbf{A}_{k+1}})
X_{\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
\xi_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}(\alpha_{[\textbf{A}]_{1,i_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{[\textbf{A}]_{k,i_{k}}})
\mu_{[\textbf{A}]_{1,i_{1}},...,[\textbf{A}]_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
\alpha_{\textbf{A}_{1,1},...,\textbf{A}_{k,i_{k}};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}
\alpha_{\textbf{A}_{1},...,\textbf{A}_{k};\textbf{A}_{k+1}}

where
X_{a,b}
denotes
X_{\textbf{A}_{a,1}},...,X_{\textbf{A}_{a,b}}
and where
[\textbf{A}]_{a,b}=(\textbf{A}_{a,1},...,\textbf{A}_{a,b};\textbf{A}_{a})
.

Remark 2.15
.

An SC functor-operad over
(\textbf{A},\textbf{B})
is a particular example of an
internal symmetric operad
in
\textbf{End}_{\textbf{A},\textbf{B}}
(the endomorphism SC type operad of
(\textbf{A},\textbf{B})
in
Cat
).
The notion of internal symmetric operad was introduced in
[
Bat08
, Definition 9.3]
.

2.5
Condensation

In this section we explain how we extend the condensation process described in
[
BB09
, Section 1]
to the case of coloured SC-operads.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a coloured SC-operad and let
\delta=(\delta_{\mathfrak{cl}},\delta_{\mathfrak{op}})
be a pair of two functors
\delta_{\mathfrak{cl}}:\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}\to\textbf{C}
and
\delta_{\mathfrak{op}}:\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{op}}\to\textbf{C}
.
We will define the
condensation operad
of
\mathcal{O}
, denoted by
\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)
,
as well as an associated functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\text{-algebra}\longrightarrow\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)\text{-algebra}.

The operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)
is obtained by condensing each type of colours into one colour, so that it is an SC type operad.
It is obtained in two steps.

From
\mathcal{O}
to the SC functor-operad
\xi(\mathcal{O})
.

Recall that, by the hypotheses H2 and H3 from Definition
2.11
, both
\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{op}}
are
C
-categories. Moreover, the category
\textbf{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}}
(resp.
\textbf{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{op}}}
) of
C
-functors from
\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}
(resp. from
\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}
) to
C
is a
C
-category.
For
k\geq 0
and
(c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1})
a tuple of elements in
\{\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op}\}
satisfying

(2.2)

c_{k+1}=\mathfrak{op}\text{ if there exists $1\leq i\leq k$ such that $c_{i}=\mathfrak{op}$},

one lets
\textbf{A}_{i}:=\textbf{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{u}^{c_{i}}}
and one defines the
C
-functor

\displaystyle\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}:\textbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\textbf{A}_{k}\to\textbf{A}_{k+1}

as the coend

\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(X_{c_{1}},...,X_{c_{k}})(n)=\mathcal{O}({\underbrace{-,\dots,-}_{k}};n)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{u}^{c_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{u}^{c_{k}}}X_{c_{1}}(-)\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{c_{k}}(-).

We have an SC analogue to
[
BB09
, Proposition 1.8]
or
[
DS03
]
:

Proposition 2.16
.

The functors
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}
extend to an SC functor-operad
\xi(\mathcal{O})
, such that the category of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras and the category of
\xi(\mathcal{O})
-algebras are isomorphic.

Proof.

A straightforward verification, along the lines of
[
DS03
]
, shows that the family of the
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}
forms an SC functor-operad.

Via the hypothesis H2 and H3 from Definition
2.11
, an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
X
can be seen as a pair
(X_{\mathfrak{cl}},X_{\mathfrak{op}})
where
X_{\mathfrak{cl}}
and
X_{\mathfrak{op}}
are functors
X_{\mathfrak{cl}}:\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}\to\textbf{C}
and
X_{\mathfrak{op}}:\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{op}}\to\textbf{C}
respectively.
Therefore, the maps (
2.1
) induce maps

\xi_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(\mathcal{O})(X_{c_{1}},...,X_{c_{k}})(e_{k+1})=\\
\int^{e_{1},...,e_{k}}\mathcal{O}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e_{k+1})\otimes X_{c_{1}}(e_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes X_{c_{k}}(e_{k})\to X_{c_{k+1}}(e_{k+1}),

for
e_{k+1}\in Col
. They form the maps

\alpha_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}:\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(X_{c_{1}},...,X_{c_{k}})\to X_{c_{k+1}}
, for
k\geq 0
.
We conclude that
X
is a
\xi(\mathcal{O})
-algebra because of the unit, associativity and equivariance properties of maps (
2.1
). The isomorphism follows from the universal property of the coend.
∎

From
\xi(\mathcal{O})
to the coendomorphism operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)
.

The operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)
is the coendomorphism operad of the SC functor-operad
\xi(\mathcal{O})
.
Explicitly, it is given by:

\displaystyle\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)(c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1})=\Hom_{\textbf{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{u}^{c_{k+1}}}}(\delta_{c_{k+1}},\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}({\delta_{c_{1}},...,\delta_{c_{k}}})),

for
(c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1})
satisfying (
2.2
).
The composition maps

\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)(c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1})\otimes\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)(c_{1,1},...,c_{1,i_{1}};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)(c_{k,1},...,c_{k,i_{k}};c_{k})\\
\to\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)(c_{1,1},...,c_{k,i_{k}};c_{k+1})

are given by sending maps
f\otimes g_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes g_{k}
to the composite
\delta_{c_{k+1}}
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1,1},...,c_{k,i_{k}};c_{k+1}}(\delta_{c_{1,1}},...,\delta_{c_{k,i_{k}}})
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(\delta_{c_{1}},...,\delta_{c_{k}})
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(\xi(\mathcal{O})_{[c]_{1,i_{1}}}(\delta),...,\xi(\mathcal{O})_{[c]_{k,i_{k}}}(\delta))
.
f
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(g_{1},...,g_{k})
\alpha_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}

The action of
\Sigma_{k}
on
\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)
is given by post-composing with the natural transformations
\phi_{\sigma,c_{1},...,c_{k}}({\delta_{c_{1}},...,\delta_{c_{k}}}):\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}({\delta_{c_{1}},...,\delta_{c_{k}}})\to\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},...,c_{\sigma^{-1}(k)};c_{k+1}}({\delta_{c_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}},...,\delta_{c_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}}})
.

Given an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
X=(X_{\mathfrak{cl}},X_{\mathfrak{op}})
, we set

\displaystyle Tot_{\delta_{\mathfrak{cl}}}X_{\mathfrak{cl}}:=\Hom_{\textbf{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}}}(\delta_{\mathfrak{cl}},X_{\mathfrak{cl}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Tot_{\delta_{\mathfrak{op}}}X_{\mathfrak{op}}:=\Hom_{\textbf{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{u}^{\mathfrak{op}}}}(\delta_{\mathfrak{op}},X_{\mathfrak{op}}).

Since
X
is a
\xi(\mathcal{O})
-algebra by Proposition
2.16
and since
\Coend_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)
is the coendomorphism operad of
\xi(\mathcal{O})
, it follows that the pair
(Tot_{\delta_{\mathfrak{cl}}}X_{\mathfrak{cl}},Tot_{\delta_{\mathfrak{op}}}X_{\mathfrak{op}})
is a
{\Coend}_{\mathcal{O}}(\delta)
-algebra:
the action maps

(2.3)

\Coend_{\xi(\mathcal{O})}(\delta)(c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1})\otimes Tot_{\delta_{c_{1}}}X_{c_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes Tot_{\delta_{c_{k}}}X_{c_{k}}\to Tot_{\delta_{c_{k+1}}}X_{c_{k+1}}

are given by sending maps
f\otimes g_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes g_{k}
to the composite
\delta_{c_{k+1}}
X_{c_{k+1}}
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(\delta_{c_{1}},...,\delta_{c_{k}})
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(X_{c_{1}},...,X_{c_{k}})
.
f
\xi(\mathcal{O})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}(g_{1},...,g_{k})
\alpha_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c_{k+1}}

Unit, associative and equivariance properties of the maps (
2.3
) are deduced from the SC functor-operad properties of
\xi(\mathcal{O})
.

3
Cellular decompositions of the Swiss Cheese operad

The little cubes operad
\mathcal{C}
has a cellular decomposition indexed by the extended complete graph operad
\mathcal{K}
, see
[
Ber97
]
and
[
BFV07
, 4.1]
.
We extend this result to the Swiss Cheese operads
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
,
m\geq 1
what provides a recognition principle for SC type operads.
In particular, we construct a poset operad
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
that indexes the cells
(\mathcal{SC}_{m})^{(\alpha)}
of
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
.
This leads to a zig-zag of weak equivalences of operads

\mathcal{SC}_{m}
\hocolim_{\alpha\in\mathcal{RK}_{m}}(\mathcal{SC}_{m})^{(\alpha)}
\Ba\mathcal{RK}_{m}
,
\sim
\sim

between the Swiss Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
and the operad of the geometric realization of the nerve of
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
.
Besides, there is a second way to index the cells
(\mathcal{SC}_{m})^{(\alpha)}
; this is done by another poset operad
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
, providing a similar zig-zag.

3.1
The Swiss Cheese operad

The Swiss Cheese operad that we use is the cubical version of the one defined in
[
Kon99
]
.

Let
m\geq 1
. Let
Sym:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the reflection
Sym(x_{1},...x_{m})=(x_{1},...,-x_{m})
, and let
Half_{+}
be the upper half space

Half_{+}=\{(x_{1},...,x_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}|x_{m}>0\}.

The standard cube
C_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
is
C_{0}=[-1,1]^{\times m}
.
A cube
C
in the standard cube is of the form
{C}=[x_{1},y_{1}]\times[x_{2},y_{2}]\times\dots\times[x_{m},y_{m}]
with
-1<x_{j}<y_{j}<1
for
1\leq j\leq m
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
n\geq 0
and
c_{i},c\in\{\mathfrak{cl},\mathfrak{op}\}
we define a topological
\Sigma_{n}
-space
\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)
as
the empty-set if
c=\mathfrak{cl}
and there exists
1\leq i\leq n
such that
c_{i}=\mathfrak{op}
; for the other cases, it is

•

the space of the little
m
-cubes operad
\mathcal{C}^{(m)}(n)
defined in
[
May72
]
for
c=\mathfrak{cl}
;

•

the empty set if
n=0
;

•

the one-point space if
n=1
;

•

in the case
s+t=n\geq 2
with
s,t\geq 0
such that
s
colours
c_{i}
are
\mathfrak{cl}
and
t
colours
c_{j}
are
\mathfrak{op}
, the space of configuration of
2s+t
disjoint cubes
(C_{1},...,C_{2s+t})
in the standard cube
C_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
Sym(C_{i})=C_{i+s}
for
1\leq i\leq s
and
Sym(C_{i})=C_{i}
for
2s+1\leq i\leq 2s+t
and such that all the cubes
(C_{1},...,C_{s})
are in the upper half space.

Remark 3.2
.

Because of the symmetry conditions imposed by
Sym
, we may think of
\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{op})
as the configuration space of cubes
(C_{1},...,C_{s})
and semi-cubes
(C_{s+1},...,C_{s+t})
lying in the standard semi-cube
Half_{+}\cap C_{0}
.

Similarly to the little
m
-cubes operad
\mathcal{C}^{(m)}
the composition maps

\displaystyle\circ_{i}:\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)\times\mathcal{SC}_{m}(d_{1},...,d_{r};c_{i})\to\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{i-1},d_{1},...,d_{r},c_{i+1},...,c_{n};c)

are defined as substitutions of cubes.
We denote the resulting SC type operad by
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
.

3.2
The SC extended complete graph operad

We define the
SC (or relative) extended complete graph
operad
\mathcal{RK}
.
It is an SC type operad in the category of posets. We provide two filtrations by sub-operads
\{\mathcal{RK}_{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
and
\{\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
. Their closed part are isomorphic to
\mathcal{K}_{m}
and their open part are isomorphic to
\mathcal{K}_{m-1}
, where
\{\mathcal{K}_{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
denotes the extended complete graph operad defined in
[
BFV07
, Section 4.1]
.

3.2.1
Definition of
\mathcal{RK}

Given
n
colours
c_{i}\in\{\mathfrak{cl},\mathfrak{op}\}
, we denote by
\{\widetilde{c}_{1},...,\widetilde{c}_{n}\}
the set with

(3.1)

\widetilde{c}_{i}=\begin{cases}i&\text{ if }c_{i}=\mathfrak{cl};\\
\underline{i}&\text{ if }c_{i}=\mathfrak{op}.\end{cases}

A colouring and an orientation
(\mu,\sigma)
on the complete graph on
\{\widetilde{c}_{1},...,\widetilde{c}_{n}\}
is, for each
1\leq i<j\leq n
, a strict positive natural number
\mu_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}^{>0}
and an orientation
\sigma_{i,j}
(that is,
\widetilde{c}_{i}\to\widetilde{c}_{j}
or
\widetilde{c}_{i}\leftarrow\widetilde{c}_{j}
).
A
monochromatic acyclic orientation
of a complete graph is a colouring and an orientation such that there exist no oriented cycles with the same colour,
i.e.
there are no configurations of the form
\widetilde{c}_{i_{1}}\to\widetilde{c}_{i_{2}}\to\cdots\to\widetilde{c}_{i_{k}}\to\widetilde{c}_{i_{1}}
with
\mu_{i_{1},i_{2}}=\mu_{i_{2},i_{3}}=\cdots=\mu_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}=\mu_{i_{k},i_{1}}
.
A
marked
monochromatic acyclic orientation
(\mu,\sigma)^{c}
is a monochromatic acyclic orientation
(\mu,\sigma)
together with a colour
c\in\{\mathfrak{cl},\mathfrak{op}\}
.

If there exists an
i
such that
c_{i}=\mathfrak{op}
, then we define
\mathcal{RK}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{cl})
as the empty set.
Otherwise,
\mathcal{RK}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)
is the set of the marked monochromatic acyclic orientations
(\mu,\sigma)^{c}
of the complete graph on
\{\widetilde{c}_{1},...,\widetilde{c}_{n}\}
.

The poset structure is given by

(\mu,\sigma)^{c}\leq(\mu^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})^{c}\Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \forall i<j,\text{ or }(\mu_{i,j},\sigma_{i,j})=(\mu^{\prime}_{i,j},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,j})\penalty\ \text{or }\mu_{i,j}<\mu^{\prime}_{i,j}.

Given a permutation
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
and an element
(\mu,\tau)^{c}\in\mathcal{RK}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)
, the resulting element
\sigma\cdot(\mu,\tau)^{c}\in\mathcal{RK}(c_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},...,c_{\sigma^{-1}(n)};c)
is given by permuting the numbers
i
by
\sigma
without changing neither the underline nor the orientation nor the colouring. For example,
the edges
\underline{i}\to j
of
(\mu,\tau)^{c}
with colours
\mu_{i,j}
become the edges
\underline{\sigma(i)}\to\sigma(j)
with the same colours
\mu_{i,j}
.

The compositions

\gamma^{\mathcal{RK}}:\mathcal{RK}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)\times\mathcal{RK}(c_{1,1},...,c_{1,k_{1}};c_{1})\times...\times\mathcal{RK}(c_{n,1},...,c_{n,k_{n}};c_{n})\to\mathcal{RK}(c_{1,1},...,c_{n,k_{n}};c)

send a tuple
(\alpha;\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
of
\mathcal{RK}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)\times\mathcal{RK}(c_{1,1},...,c_{1,k_{1}};c_{1})\times\dots\times\mathcal{RK}(c_{n,1},...,c_{n,k_{n}};c_{n})
to an element in
\mathcal{RK}(c_{1,1},...,c_{n,k_{n}};c)
obtained as follows.
The sub complete graphs with vertices in the same block
\{c_{i,1},...,c_{i,k_{i}}\}
is oriented and coloured as
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{RK}(c_{i,1},...,c_{i,k_{i}};c_{i})
; the edges with vertices in two different blocks are oriented and coloured as the edges between the corresponding vertices in
\alpha\in\mathcal{RK}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)
.
For example,

\gamma^{\mathcal{RK}}\left(\hbox to55.42pt{\vbox to32.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-12.02156pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

3.2.2
Filtrations of
\mathcal{RK}

We define two different filtrations of
\mathcal{RK}
by sub-operads
(\mathcal{RK}_{m})_{m\geq 1}
and
(\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m})_{m\geq 1}
.

For
m\geq 1
, the sub-operad
\mathcal{RK}_{m}\subset\mathcal{RK}
is defined as follows.
The closed part is defined by

\mathcal{RK}_{m}(\mathfrak{cl},...,\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{cl})=\{(\mu,\sigma)^{\mathfrak{cl}}\in\mathcal{RK}(\mathfrak{cl},...,\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{cl})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ i<j\}.

The non-closed part is defined, for an
(n+1)
-tuple of colours
(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{op})
, by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{op})=\{(\mu,\sigma)^{\mathfrak{op}}\in\mathcal{RK}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{op})\penalty\ |

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m

\displaystyle\text{ if }c_{i}=c_{j}=\mathfrak{cl},

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m-1

\displaystyle\text{ if }c_{i}=c_{j}=\mathfrak{op},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m-1

\displaystyle\text{ if }\underline{i}\to j\text{ or }i\leftarrow\underline{j}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m

\displaystyle\text{ if }i\to\underline{j}\text{ or }\underline{i}\leftarrow{j}\}.

The second sub-operad
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}\subset\mathcal{RK}
is obtained by exchanging the last two conditions on
\mu_{i,j}
above.
Explicitly,

\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}(\mathfrak{cl},...,\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{cl})=\mathcal{RK}_{m}(\mathfrak{cl},...,\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{cl}),

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{op})=\{(\mu,\sigma)^{\mathfrak{op}}\in\mathcal{RK}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{op})\penalty\ |

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m

\displaystyle\text{ if }c_{i}=c_{j}=\mathfrak{cl},

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m-1

\displaystyle\text{ if }c_{i}=c_{j}=\mathfrak{op},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m

\displaystyle\text{ if }\underline{i}\to j\text{ or }i\leftarrow\underline{j}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mu_{i,j}\leq m-1

\displaystyle\text{ if }i\to\underline{j}\text{ or }\underline{i}\leftarrow{j}\}.

Remark 3.3
.

For
m=1
the conditions where
\mu_{i,j}\leq m-1
cannot be satisfied. It follows that
\mathcal{RK}_{1}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{op})
and
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{1}(c_{1},...,c_{n};\mathfrak{op})
are empty whenever the tuple
(c_{1},...,c_{n})
has more than one open colour.

3.3
Cellular decompositions of the Swiss Cheese operad

The idea of cellular decomposition of operads comes from
[
Ber97
]
. It consists in a cellular decomposition of each spaces that is compatible with the operad structure.

Recall from
[
BB09
, 3.1]
that, given a topological space
X
and a poset
\mathcal{A}
,
an
\mathcal{A}
-
cellulation
of
X
is a functor
\Theta:\mathcal{A}\to\textbf{Top}
such that:

1.

\colim_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\Theta(\alpha)\cong X
;

2.

for each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, the canonical map
\colim_{\beta<\alpha}\Theta(\beta)\to\Theta(\alpha)
is a closed cofibration;

3.

for each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, the “cell“
\Theta(\alpha)
is contractible.

Such an
\mathcal{A}
-cellulation provides a zig-zag of weak equivalences:

(3.4)

X\cong\colim_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\Theta(\alpha)\longleftarrow\hocolim_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\Theta(\alpha)\longrightarrow\hocolim_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}(*)\cong\Ba\mathcal{A},

where
\Ba\mathcal{A}
denotes the realization of the nerve of the category
\mathcal{A}
.
Moreover, if
X
and
\mathcal{A}
are operads and if the cellular decomposition of
X
is compatible with its operadic structure, then all the objects in (
3.4
) are operads and the weak equivalences are morphisms of operads. It is straightforward to check that this holds for SC type operads for which the notion of a compatible cellular decomposition is as follows.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a poset SC type operad.
A topological SC type operad
\mathcal{O}
is called an
\mathcal{A}
-
cellular operad
if, for each
(n+1)
-tuple of colours
(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)
, there is an
\mathcal{A}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)
-cellulation of
\mathcal{O}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)
,

\Theta_{c_{1},...,c_{n};c}:\mathcal{A}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)\to\textbf{Top},

subject to the following two compatibilities.

1.

Compatibility with the
\Sigma_{n}
-action:

\Theta_{c_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},...,c_{\sigma^{-1}(n)};c}(\sigma\cdot\alpha)=\sigma\cdot\Theta_{c_{1},...,c_{n};c}(\alpha)
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(c_{1},...,c_{n};c)
.

2.

Compatibility with the operadic composition:

\gamma^{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\Theta_{c_{1},...,c_{n};c}(\alpha)\times\Theta_{c_{1,1},...,c_{1,k_{1}};c_{1}}(\alpha_{1})\times\dots\times\Theta_{c_{n,1},...,c_{n,k_{n}};c_{n}}(\alpha_{n})\Big)\\
\subseteq\Theta_{c_{1,1},...,c_{n,k_{n}};c}\left(\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}(\alpha;\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})\right),

for all variables
c,c_{i},c_{i,j},\alpha,\alpha_{i}
, where
\gamma^{\mathcal{O}}
and
\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}
denote the composition maps of
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{A}
respectively.

In what follows, one shows that Berger’s cellular decomposition of the little
m
-cubes operad (see
[
Ber97
, Theorem 1.16]
and
[
BFV07
]
) extends to
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
m\geq 1
. The Swiss Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
has the structure of an
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
-cellular operad as well as the structure of an
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
-cellular operad.

Proof.

We start by the
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
-cellular decomposition; we explain at the end of the proof how to proceed for the other decomposition.

We use the description of
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
via cubes and semi-cubes given in Remark
3.2
. The integer
m\geq 1
is fixed.

Notation:

For
C_{1}
either a cube or a semi-cube and
C_{2}
either a cube or a semi-cube, we write
C_{1}\square_{\mu}C_{2}
if they are separated by a hyperplane
H_{i}
orthogonal to the
i
-th coordinate axis for some
i\leq\mu
, such that whenever there is no separating hyperplane
H_{i}
for
i<\mu
, the left element
C_{1}
lies in the
negative
side of
H_{\mu}
and
C_{2}
lies in the
positive
side of
H_{\mu}
.

For
\alpha=(\mu,\sigma)^{c}\in\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
, we define
\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)^{(\alpha)}
to be the cell

\displaystyle\{(C_{1},...,C_{k})\in\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)\penalty\ |\penalty\ C_{i}\square_{\mu_{i,j}}C_{j}\text{ if }\widetilde{c}_{i}\to\widetilde{c}_{j}\text{ and }C_{j}\square_{\mu_{i,j}}C_{i}\text{ if }\widetilde{c}_{i}\leftarrow\widetilde{c}_{j}\}.

For example, consider the following configurations in
\mathcal{SC}_{2}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})
,

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\text{ that form }\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{2}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op}).

The cell
\mathcal{SC}_{2}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})^{(\alpha)}
is made of configurations of type
X
; the cell

\mathcal{SC}_{2}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})^{(\beta)}
is made of configurations of type
Y
; and,
the cell
\mathcal{SC}_{2}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})^{(\gamma)}
is made of configurations of type
X
,
Y
and configurations of type
Z
(which intersect with configurations of type
X
or
Y
whenever
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are separated by
2
hyperplanes
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
).

As a remark, note that in
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
, whenever
C_{1}
is a semi-cube and
C_{2}
is a cube, if
H_{m}
exists, then
C_{1}
lies in the negative side of
H_{m}
(e.g.
Z
).
From this one can see that, with this definition of the cells, the condition
\mu_{i,j}\leq m-1
if
i\to\underline{j}
given in the definition of
\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
is necessary for ensuring the contractibility of the cells.
Indeed, if one removes the above condition, then there exists
\delta_{m}=\hbox to55.42pt{\vbox to31.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-10.31044pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

that indexes the space
\mathcal{SC}_{m}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})^{(\delta_{m})}
, which turns out to be homotopic to the
m-2
-sphere (for
m=2
, one has

\mathcal{SC}_{2}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})^{(\delta_{2})}=\mathcal{SC}_{2}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})^{(\alpha)}\sqcup\mathcal{SC}_{2}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})^{(\beta)}
).

Our definition recovers that of Berger when considering the open part and the closed part of
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
separately.
The main input of our construction, then, resides in the interaction between cubes and semi-cubes.
At first sight it could appear useless to consider separating hyperplanes between cubes and semi-cubes since contractibility is not hindered by their relative positions (for example
\mathcal{SC}_{m}(\mathfrak{op},\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op})
is contractible).
However it is important to do so for the operadic composition of the cells.
This is because, in a configuration of cubes and semi-cubes, if one substitutes a semi-cube, then a cube may appear; the position of such a cube has to be compared with that of the other cubes.
For instance, consider the following substitution:

then, in the resulting configuration (the right-hand side standard semi-cube), knowing the position of
2
relative to
3
and
4
requires the knowledge of the position of
\underline{1}
relative to
2
and
3
in the first term (the left-hand side standard semi-cube).

For the contractibility of the cells, both closed and open cells are known as being contractible; the same argument as
[
Ber97
, Theorem 1.16]
shows that open/closed cells also are.

The fact that
\colim_{\alpha\in\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)}\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)^{(\alpha)}\cong\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
, essentially follows from the
2
following facts.

•

If
x\in\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
then there exists an
\alpha\in\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
such that
x\in\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)^{(\alpha)}
. This is immediate since any two cubes/semi-cubes are separated by a hyperplane. For example, if
C_{i}
is a semi-cube and
C_{j}
a cube, then
\alpha=(\mu,\sigma)^{c}
may be chosen such that
\mu_{i,j}=m
and
\sigma_{i,j}=\underline{i}\to j
.

•

If
\alpha=(\mu,\sigma)^{c}
and
\beta=(\mu^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})^{c}
are incomparable (neither
\alpha\leq\beta
nor
\alpha\geq\beta
) and are such that there exists
x=(C_{1},...,C_{k})\in\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)^{(\alpha)}\cap\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)^{(\beta)}
, then
x\in\mathcal{SC}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)^{(\gamma)}
for a
\gamma
such that
\gamma<\alpha
and
\gamma<\beta
.
This follows from the observation that, for each
i<j
such that
(\mu_{i,j},\sigma_{i,j})
and
(\mu^{\prime}_{i,j},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,j})
are not comparable, there exists
\mu^{\prime\prime}_{i,j}<\mu_{i,j}=\mu^{\prime}_{i,j}
such that
C_{i}\square_{\mu^{\prime\prime}_{i,j}}C_{j}
or
C_{j}\square_{\mu^{\prime\prime}_{i,j}}C_{i}
; let us denote by
\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i,j}
the corresponding orientation. The element
\gamma=(\mu^{\prime\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime})^{c}
is given by
(\mu^{\prime\prime}_{i,j},\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i,j})
as above whenever
i<j
indexes not comparable components and by
(\mu^{\prime\prime}_{i,j},\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i,j})=\min\{(\mu_{i,j},\sigma_{i,j}),(\mu^{\prime}_{i,j},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,j})\}
otherwise.

The other cellular decomposition (indexed by
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
) is obtained by exchanging, in
Notation
above, the terms
negative
and
positive
. In what concerns the closed and the open parts, such an exchange is not relevant. However, as we have remarked before, whenever
C_{1}
is a semi-cube and
C_{2}
is a cube, if
H_{m}
exists, then
C_{1}
lies in the negative side of
H_{m}
, hence
the condition
\mu_{i,j}\leq m-1
if
i\leftarrow\underline{j}
given in the definition of
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
.
∎

4
The operad
\mathcal{RL}

4.1
Definition of the operad
\mathcal{RL}

We describe a coloured SC-operad
\mathcal{RL}
in the category of sets,
Set
.

The operad
\mathcal{RL}
has two natural filtrations by sub operads
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
for
m\geq 1
. For each
m\geq 1
, we can think of
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
(resp.
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
) as mixes between the sub operads
\mathcal{L}_{m}
and
\mathcal{L}_{m-1}
of the lattice paths operad
\mathcal{L}
introduced in
[
BB09
]
.
The operad
\mathcal{RL}
has
2
types of colours, the closed colours
\mathbb{N}
and the open colours
\underline{\mathbb{N}}
, while
\mathcal{L}
has one type of colours
\mathbb{N}
.

We generalize
[
BB09
, Section 2]
which serves as a basis for this section. In particular we refer to
loc. cit.
for the definition of the category of bipointed small categories
\textbf{Cat}_{*,*}
, ordinals
[n]
and for the tensor product of ordinals
[i]\otimes[j]
. Decorating each object of the ordinal
[n]
with an underline gives the
underlined ordinal

[\underline{n}]
.
One denotes by
\underline{\mathbb{N}}
the set of the natural numbers decorated by an underline; for
\underline{n}\in\underline{\mathbb{N}}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-n}
one lets
\underline{n}+k:=\underline{n+k}
.
Let us denote by
ev:\mathbb{N}\sqcup\underline{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}
the map defined by

ev(e)=n
if
e=n
or
e=\underline{n}
.

Definition of
\mathcal{RL}
.

The set of colours of
\mathcal{RL}
is
Col=Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}\sqcup Col_{\mathfrak{op}}

where
Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}:=\mathbb{N}
and
Col_{\mathfrak{op}}:=\underline{\mathbb{N}}
. Hence,
n\in Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}
whereas
\underline{n}\in Col_{\mathfrak{op}}
.

For a
(k+1)
-tuple of colours
(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
in
Col
, the set
\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
is defined as:

•

the empty set
\emptyset
, if
e\in Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}
and if there is an
i
such that
e_{i}\in Col_{\mathfrak{op}}
;

•

\textbf{Cat}_{*,*}([e+1],[e_{1}+1]\otimes[e_{2}+1]\otimes\dots\otimes[e_{k}+1])
, otherwise.

The substitutions maps are the natural extension to that of
\mathcal{L}
, that is, they are given by tensor and composition in
\textbf{Cat}_{*,*}
.

Remark 4.1
.

Accordingly, one recovers the lattice path operad,

\displaystyle\mathcal{RL}(n_{1},...,n_{k};n)=\mathcal{L}(n_{1},...,n_{k};n)=\textbf{Cat}_{*,*}([n+1],[n_{1}+1]\otimes[n_{2}+1]\otimes\dots\otimes[n_{k}+1]),

for all
(k+1)
-tuple
(n_{1},...,n_{k};n)
of colours in
Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}=\mathbb{N}
.

For instance, an element
x\in\mathcal{RL}(n_{1},\underline{n_{2}};\underline{n})

is a functor
x:[\underline{n+1}]\to[n_{1}+1]\otimes[\underline{n_{2}+1}]
that sends
(0,\underline{n+1})
on
((0,\underline{0}),(n_{1}+1,\underline{n_{2}+1}))
and is determined by the image of the
n
remaining objects of
[\underline{n+1}]
and the morphisms into the lattice
[n_{1}+1]\otimes[\underline{n_{2}+1}]
.

Example 4.2
.

The following lattice path
x
belongs to
\mathcal{RL}(3,\underline{2};\underline{3})
:

(4.1)

(0,\underline{3})
\bullet
x(\underline{4})
\cdot
\bullet
\bullet
x(\underline{2})=x(\underline{3})
x(\underline{1})
x(\underline{0})
\bullet
(4,\underline{0})
1
\underline{2}
\underline{2}
1
1
\underline{2}
1

Integer-strings representation.

In
[
BB09
, paragraph 2.2]
a description of
\mathcal{L}
in terms of integer-strings is given.
One has the obvious similar bijective correspondence for
\mathcal{RL}
, where one considers natural numbers and underlined natural numbers; the latter correspond to the open colours.
Additionally, we put an extra label according to the nature of the output colour.
Explicitly, given a lattice path
x\in\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
, one runs through it starting from
x(0)
(or
x(\underline{0})
) and ending at
x(e+1)
. Along the way, each time one meets an edge that is parallel to the
i
-axis one writes down
i
if
e_{i}\in Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}
and
\underline{i}
if
e_{i}\in Col_{\mathfrak{op}}
; one writes down a vertical bar each time one meets an
x(a)
for
1\leq ev(a)\leq ev(e)
. One adds an
\mathfrak{op}
if the output colour belongs to
Col_{\mathfrak{op}}
.

The
ev(e)
vertical bars of the integer-string
x
subdivide it into
ev(e)+1
(possibly empty)
substrings
.
The substrings are indexed by
[ev(e)]
.

Example 4.3
.

The lattice path
x
from (
4.1
) corresponds to the integer-string
(1\underline{2}|\underline{2}11||\underline{2}1)^{\mathfrak{op}}
.

Example 4.4
.

(121)^{\mathfrak{op}}\in\mathcal{RL}(1,0;\underline{0})
whereas
(121)\in\mathcal{RL}(1,0;0)
.

Let us exhibit the corresponding composition on integer-string representations via an example.

Example 4.5
.

(1\underline{2}|1\underline{4}\underline{2}31||\underline{2}\underline{4})^{\mathfrak{op}}\circ_{\underline{2}}(1\underline{3}|21\underline{3}|\underline{3}1)^{\mathfrak{op}}=(1{2\underline{4}}|1\underline{6}{32\underline{4}}51||{\underline{4}2}\underline{6})^{\mathfrak{op}}.

We have renumbered the integer-string
(1\underline{2}|1\underline{4}\underline{2}31||\underline{2}\underline{4})^{\mathfrak{op}}
by increasing the integers greater than
\underline{2}
by
2
(=one less than the number of integers in the second integer-string):
(1\underline{2}|1\underline{6}\underline{2}51||\underline{2}\underline{6})^{\mathfrak{op}}
. We have increased the integers of the second integer-string by
1
(= one less than the value of
\underline{2}
):
(2\underline{4}|32\underline{4}|\underline{4}2)^{\mathfrak{op}}
. Finally, we have has substituted the three occurrences of
\underline{2}
by the three substrings
2\underline{4}
,
32\underline{4}
and
\underline{4}2
.

The action of the symmetric group
\sigma\cdot x\in\mathcal{RL}(e_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},...,e_{\sigma^{-1}(k)};e)
is obtained by permuting the number
i
(resp.
\underline{i}
) of the string-integer representation of
x
by the number
\sigma(i)
(resp.
\underline{\sigma(i)}
).

Example 4.6
.

For
x=(1\underline{2}|3\underline{2}11||\underline{2}1)^{\mathfrak{op}}
and
\sigma(1)=2
,
\sigma(2)=3
,
\sigma(3)=1
,
one has
\sigma\cdot x=(2\underline{3}|1\underline{3}22||\underline{3}2)^{\mathfrak{op}}
.

The underlying category of
\mathcal{RL}
.

It follows directly from
[
BB09
, paragraph 2.4]
and the definition of
\mathcal{RL}
that
the two underlying sub-categories
(\mathcal{RL})_{u}^{\mathfrak{cl}}
and
(\mathcal{RL})_{u}^{\mathfrak{op}}
are (canonically isomorphic to) the simplicial category
\triangle
.
Thus, for each
k\geq 0
, one has a functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{RL}(-,...,-;-):(\triangle^{\text{op}})^{k}\times\triangle\to\textbf{Set},

that is, a multisimplicial/cosimplicial set.

Dual interpretation of
\mathcal{RL}
.

For later use, let us mention that
\mathcal{RL}
has a dual interpretation as given in
[
BB09
, Lemma 2.7]
. In particular, an element
x\in\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
determines a
k
-tuple
(x_{1},...,x_{k})\in\triangle([e_{1}],[e])\times\cdots\times\triangle([e_{k}],[e])
.
The value of
x_{i}(r)
corresponds to the substring of the integer-string
x
in which the
r+1
-th occurrence of
i
(or
\underline{i}
) appears. We refer to
loc. cit.
for more details.

Example 4.7
.

The integer-string
x
from Example
4.3
determines the pair
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\triangle([3],[\underline{3}])\times\triangle([\underline{2}],[\underline{3}])
given by

x_{1}:(0,1,2,3)\mapsto(\underline{0},\underline{1},\underline{1},\underline{3})\text{ and }x_{2}:(\underline{0},\underline{1},\underline{2})\mapsto(\underline{0},\underline{1},\underline{3})
.

Filtrations of
\mathcal{RL}
by sub operads.

Let us define two maps

c_{i,j},\hat{c}_{i,j}:\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)\to\mathbb{N}.

The map
c_{i,j}
is defined as in
[
BB09
, paragraph 2.9]
and will be used to defined the complexity index for the closed and the open parts of
\mathcal{RL}
. The map
\hat{c}_{i,j}
will be used to control the interaction between the closed and the open part.

For
1\leq i<j\leq k
, we denote by

\phi_{ij}:\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)\to\mathcal{RL}(e_{i},e_{j};e)

the projection induced by the canonical projection

p_{ij}:[e_{1}+1]\otimes\cdots\otimes[e_{k}+1]\to[e_{i}+1]\otimes[e_{j}+1].

For
x\in\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
and
1\leq i<j\leq k
, we define
c_{ij}(x)
as the number of changes of directions in the lattice paths
\phi_{ij}(x)
.

The second number
\hat{c}_{i,j}(x)
is defined as follows.
Recall that if
x\in\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
, then its integer-string representation is, in particular, a sequence of numbers (underlined or not) between
1
and
k
. For
1\leq i\leq k
, we set
i^{-}
(resp.
\underline{i}^{-}
) to be the first occurrence of
i
(resp.
\underline{i}
) in the integer-string representation. Equivalently,
i^{-}
(resp.
\underline{i}^{-}
) is the first edge of the lattice
x
which is in the
i
-th direction.
We write
r^{-}<s^{-}
if the element
r^{-}
precedes
s^{-}
.

For
1\leq i<j\leq k
, we set:

(4.2)

\hat{c}_{i,j}(x)=\begin{cases}c_{i,j}(x)&\text{ if }\penalty\ i^{-}<\underline{j}^{-};\\
c_{i,j}(x)+1&\text{ if }\penalty\ i^{-}>\underline{j}^{-};\\
c_{i,j}(x)+1&\text{ if }\penalty\ \underline{i}^{-}<j^{-};\\
c_{i,j}(x)&\text{ if }\penalty\ \underline{i}^{-}>j^{-}.\end{cases}

For
m\geq 1
, we define
\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
as the set of elements
x\in\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
satisfying the three conditions:

\displaystyle\max_{(i,j)}c_{i,j}(x)\leq m,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \max_{(\underline{i},\underline{j})}c_{i,j}(x)\leq m-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\max_{(\underline{i},j)\text{ or }(i,\underline{j})}\hat{c}_{i,j}(x)\leq m.

Changing the number defined in (
4.2
) to:

(4.3)

\hat{c}^{\prime}_{i,j}(x)=\begin{cases}c_{i,j}(x)+1&\text{ if }\penalty\ i^{-}<\underline{j}^{-};\\
c_{i,j}(x)&\text{ if }\penalty\ i^{-}>\underline{j}^{-};\\
c_{i,j}(x)&\text{ if }\penalty\ \underline{i}^{-}<j^{-};\\
c_{i,j}(x)+1&\text{ if }\penalty\ \underline{i}^{-}>j^{-},\end{cases}

provides another filtration of
\mathcal{RL}
by sub-operads
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
. Explicitly,

\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
is the set of elements
x\in\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
satisfying the three conditions:

\displaystyle\max_{(i,j)}c_{i,j}(x)\leq m,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \max_{(\underline{i},\underline{j})}c_{i,j}(x)\leq m-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\max_{(\underline{i},j)\text{ or }(i,\underline{j})}\hat{c}^{\prime}_{i,j}(x)\leq m.

A relation between these two filtrations and the two cellular decompositions of
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
from Theorem
3.5
is given in the next section.

4.2
The operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta)
as an SC type operad

Given a functor
\delta:\bigtriangleup\to\textbf{C}
where
C
is a
monoidal model category
, by following the method developed in
[
BB09
, Sections 3.5-3.6]
, we construct a zig-zag of weak equivalences of operads

(4.4)

\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta)\longleftarrow\Coend_{\widehat{\mathcal{RL}}_{m}}(\delta)\longrightarrow\Ba_{\delta}\mathcal{RK}_{m},

whenever
\delta
satisfies some conditions. Here,
\Ba_{\delta}\mathcal{A}
denotes the
\delta
-realization of the nerve of the category
\mathcal{A}
.
The intermediate operad
\widehat{\mathcal{RL}}_{m}
is defined using homotopy colimits in
C
applied on a decomposition of
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
indexed by
\mathcal{RK}_{m}
.

We use most of the material from and the same conventions as in
[
BB09
, Sections 3.5-3.6]
.
In particular, we require
\delta
to be a
standard system of simplices
. This confers on homotopy colimits good properties, such as compatibility with the symmetric monoidal structure of
C
.
The following functors
\delta_{yon}
,
\delta_{\text{Top}}
and
\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}
are such standard system of simplices.

Let

\delta_{Top}:\triangle
\textbf{Set}^{\bigtriangleup^{\text{op}}}
Top
\delta_{yon}
|-|

and

\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}:\triangle
Set
{}^{\bigtriangleup^{\text{op}}}
\textbf{Ch}(\mathbb{Z})
\delta_{yon}
C_{*}(-;\mathbb{Z})

be the two functors where:

•

\delta_{yon}([n])=\Hom_{\triangle}(-,[n])
is the Yoneda functor;

•

|-|:\textbf{Set}^{\bigtriangleup^{\text{op}}}\to\textbf{Top}
is the geometric realization; and,

•

C_{*}(-;\mathbb{Z}):\textbf{Set}^{\bigtriangleup^{\text{op}}}\to\textbf{Ch}(\mathbb{Z})
is the normalized chain complex.

Let us recall that for
\delta=(\delta_{\mathfrak{cl}},\delta_{\mathfrak{op}})
with
\delta_{\mathfrak{cl}},\delta_{\mathfrak{op}}:\bigtriangleup\to\textbf{C}
, the functor
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)
denotes the
\delta
-realization:

\displaystyle\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)(n)=\mathcal{RL}_{m}(\underbrace{-,\dots,-}_{k};n)\otimes_{\bigtriangleup^{k}}\delta_{c_{1}}(-)\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_{c_{k}}(-),

where we use implicitly the strong monoidal functor
\textbf{Set}\to\textbf{C}
,
E\mapsto\coprod_{e\in E}1_{\textbf{C}}
.

We use the same functor
\delta_{\mathfrak{cl}}=\delta_{\mathfrak{op}}
and we denote it by
\delta
. This way, by using condensation from Section
2.5
, we define the SC type operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta)
, and similarly for
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta)
.

The idea for providing the zig-zag (
4.4
) follows that of (
3.4
) and relies on
a decomposition of
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)
by “cells”
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)
indexed by the
\alpha\in\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
.
Under some properties, the right-sided weak equivalence results from “the contraction of the cells”, that is, from weak equivalences
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)\to I
, where
I
denotes the constant cosimplicial object
I^{n}=1_{\textbf{C}}
; the left-sided weak equivalence is induced by the natural map
\hocolim\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)\to\colim\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)
.

In fact, Batanin and Berger
[
BB09
, Theorem 3.8]
show that, in the closed case, (
4.4
) holds provided that
\mathcal{L}_{m}
is
strongly
\delta
-reductive
.
Here we extend their result to
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
. For consistency we recall the notion of
strong
\delta
-reductivity
in our context. Let us also recall that a weak equivalence in
C
is called
universal
if any pullback of it is again a weak equivalence.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\delta
be a standard system of simplices in
C
.
The operad
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
is called
\delta
-reductive
if for any
n\geq 0
and
k\geq 0
and any colours
c_{i},c\in\{\mathfrak{cl};\mathfrak{op}\}
satisfying (
2.2
), the map
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)^{n}\to\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)^{0}
is a universal weak equivalence.

The operad
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
is called
strongly
\delta
-reductive
if in addition the induced maps

\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta)(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)\to\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)^{0}
are universal weak equivalences in
C
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\delta
be a standard system of simplices in
C
.
If the operad
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
(resp.
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
) is strongly
\delta
-reductive, then the operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta)
(resp.
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta)
) is weakly equivalent to
\Ba_{\delta}\mathcal{RK}_{m}
(resp.
\Ba_{\delta}\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
).

Proof.

The cells
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)
are obtained via a map

c_{tot}:\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)\to\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c),

for each
(k+1)
-tuple of colours
(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
in
\{\mathfrak{cl},\mathfrak{op}\}
and
e_{i}\in Col_{c_{i}}
,
e\in Col_{c}
.
Such a map is defined in (
4.7
) below and recovers the maps from
[
BB09
, Proposition 3.4]
in the closed case (
i.e.

c=\mathfrak{cl}
).
Note that there is a slight inaccuracy in
[
BB09
]
since, as we will show in Example
4.12
and Lemma
4.11
, including in the closed case, the map
c_{tot}
is not a morphism of coloured operads but instead satisfies

(4.5)

c_{tot}(x\circ_{i}y)\leq c_{tot}(x)\circ_{i}c_{tot}(y).

However, such an inequality is sufficient to apply the method developed in
[
BB09
, Sections 3.5-3.6]
.
Indeed, for
\alpha\in\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
and
e_{i}\in Col_{c_{i}}
,
e\in Col_{c}
, let us define

(4.6)

(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e):=\{x\in\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e)\penalty\ |\penalty\ c_{tot}(x)\leq\alpha\}.

It follows that

\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e)=\colim_{\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)}(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e)
;
the inequality (
4.5
) ensures the compatibility of the decomposition with the operadic structures, so that it implies that this is an equality of coloured operads.
Moreover, the operad
\widehat{\mathcal{RL}}_{m}
, defined as

\widehat{\mathcal{RL}}_{m}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e)=\hocolim_{\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)}(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(e_{1},\dots,e_{k};e),

is an operad (again, because of (
4.5
) and because of the compatibility of
\hocolim
with symmetric monoidal structure).
It turns out that (
4.6
) form a multisimplicial sub-complex of
\mathcal{RL}_{m}(-,...,-;e)
, so it makes sense to take its
\delta
-realization
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)
. In fact, as cosimplicial objects,

\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)=\colim_{\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)}\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)

and
\xi(\widehat{\mathcal{RL}}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)
identifies with
\hocolim_{\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)}\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)
.

From these considerations, it is straightforward to verify that the proof of
[
BB09
, Theorem 3.8]
generalizes to our case: the
\delta
-reductivity implies that
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)\to I
is a weak equivalence; and,
via the strongly
\delta
-reductivity, the left-sided weak equivalence of the zig-zag (
4.4
) results from the weak equivalence
\hocolim\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)^{0}\to\colim\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{\alpha}(\delta)^{0}
.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

For
\delta
being
\delta_{Top}
or
\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}
, the operads
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
are strongly
\delta
-reductive.
Consequently, the operads
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta)
and
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta)
are weakly equivalent to the topological (resp. chain) Swiss Cheese operad
\mathcal{SC}_{m}
(resp.
C_{*}\mathcal{SC}_{m}
) for
\delta
being
\delta_{Top}
(resp.
\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}
).

Proof.

Again, this is a straightforward generalization of
[
BB09
, Examples 3.10]
, where it is shown that

\mathcal{L}_{m}
is strongly
\delta
-reductive for
\delta
being
\delta_{Top}
or
\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}
. The very same method applies in our context, by considering
\xi(\mathcal{RL}_{m})_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\delta)
instead of
\xi(\mathcal{L}_{m})_{k}(\delta)
.
∎

For a
(k+1)
-tuple
(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
of colours in
\{\mathfrak{cl},\mathfrak{op}\}
and
e_{i}\in Col_{c_{i}}
,
e\in Col_{c}
, let

c_{tot}:\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)\to\mathcal{RK}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c),

be as follows.
Recall the notation
\widetilde{c}_{i}
from (
3.1
).
The element
c_{tot}(x)=(\mu,\sigma)\in\mathcal{RK}_{m}
is defined, for
1\leq i<j\leq k
, by:

(4.7)

\mu_{i,j}=c_{i,j}(x)\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{c}_{i}\to\widetilde{c}_{j}&\penalty\ \text{ if }\penalty\ \widetilde{c}_{i}^{-}<\widetilde{c}_{j}^{-}\\
\widetilde{c}_{i}\leftarrow\widetilde{c}_{j}&\penalty\ \text{ if }\penalty\ \widetilde{c}_{i}^{-}>\widetilde{c}_{j}^{-}.\end{cases}

Similarly, let
c^{\prime}_{tot}:\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}\to\mathcal{RK}^{\prime}_{m}
be the map defined by the formula (
4.7
) for
x\in\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

For all
x,y\in\mathcal{RL}_{m}
and
i
such that
x\circ_{i}y
makes sense, one has the following inequality

c_{tot}(x\circ_{i}y)\leq c_{tot}(x)\circ_{i}c_{tot}(y)
. For all
x,y\in\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
and
i
such that
x\circ_{i}y
makes sense, one has the following inequality

c^{\prime}_{tot}(x\circ_{i}y)\leq c^{\prime}_{tot}(x)\circ_{i}c^{\prime}_{tot}(y)
.

Proof.

We show the first assertion, the second one is similar. In the following arguments the type of colours does not matter, so we abusively forget about the underline.
Let
x\in\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},\dots,e_{p};e)
and
y\in\mathcal{RL}_{m}(f_{1},\dots,f_{q};e_{i})
for some
1\leq i\leq p
.

For an integer
a\neq i
, one defines
a^{\prime}:=a
if
a<i
and
a^{\prime}:=a+q-1
if
a>i
.

Recall that, by definition, the complete graph
(\mu,\sigma)=c_{tot}(x)\circ_{i}c_{tot}(y)
is given by:

\displaystyle(\mu_{k+i-1,l+i-1},\sigma_{k+i-1,l+i-1})

\displaystyle=(c_{k,l}(y),\sigma_{k,l}(c_{tot}(y)))\text{ for }1\leq k<l\leq q;

\displaystyle(\mu_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}},\sigma_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=(c_{r,s}(x),\sigma_{r,s}(c_{tot}(x)))\text{ for }1\leq r<s\leq p\text{ such that }i\notin\{r,s\};

\displaystyle(\mu_{r,k+i-1},\sigma_{r,k+i-1})

\displaystyle=(c_{r,i}(x),\sigma_{r,i}(c_{tot}(x)))\text{ for }1\leq r<i\text{ and }1\leq k\leq q;

\displaystyle(\mu_{k+i-1,s^{\prime}},\sigma_{k+i-1,s^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=(c_{i,s}(x),\sigma_{i,s}(c_{tot}(x)))\text{ for }i<s\leq p\text{ and }1\leq k\leq q.

On the other hand, let us recall that the integer-string
y
is subdivided in
n_{i}+1
substrings that are delimited by the
n_{i}
vertical bars (
n_{i}:=ev(e_{i})
). Recall that the integer-string
x\circ_{i}y
is obtained by substituting the
b
-th occurrence of
i
by the
b
-th substring (indexed by
b-1
) of
y
together with a re-indexation of the values.
It follows that
c_{k+i-1,l+i-1}(x\circ_{i}y)=c_{k,l}(y)
and that the order in which the pair
((k+i-1)^{-},(l+i-1)^{-})
appears in
x\circ_{i}y
is the same as the order in which the pair
(k^{-},l^{-})
appears in
y
.
Similarly, one has
c_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(x\circ_{i}y)=c_{r,s}(x)
, and the order in which
(r^{\prime-},s^{\prime-})
appears in
x\circ_{i}y
is the same than the order in which the pair
(r^{-},s^{-})
appears in
x
.

Moreover, it is straightforward to see that
c_{r,k+i-1}(x\circ_{i}y)\leq c_{r,i}(x)
and that the equality holds if and only if
k
is present in, at least, one substring per switch between
r
and
i
. Such an equality is illustrated below: there is at least one
k
in each of the
c_{r,i}(x)
blocks of substrings

x=(\penalty\ i^{-}\penalty\ i\penalty\ \penalty\ \cdots\penalty\ \penalty\ i\penalty\ \penalty\ r^{-}\penalty\ i\penalty\ \penalty\ r\penalty\ \penalty\ \cdots\penalty\ \penalty\ r\penalty\ \penalty\ i\penalty\ \penalty\ \cdots\penalty\ \penalty\ i\penalty\ )
\phantom{,}y=(\underbrace{\overbrace{\phantom{k}}^{0}|\overbrace{{k^{-}}}^{1}|\cdots|\overbrace{\phantom{k}}}_{1}|\underbrace{\overbrace{\vphantom{p}k}}_{2}|\cdots|\underbrace{\overbrace{k}|\cdots|\overbrace{k}^{n_{i}}}_{c_{r,i}(x)})
;

here, only the integers
r
,
i
and
k
are written; the arrows indicate the substitutions.

In the equality case, the order in which
(r^{-},(k+i-1)^{-})
appears in
x\circ_{i}y
is the same as the order in which
(r^{-},i^{-})
appears in
x
.
Similar considerations hold for the inequality
c_{k+i-1,s^{\prime}}(x\circ_{i}y)\leq c_{i,s}(x)
.

It follows from the above paragraphs that
c_{tot}(x\circ_{i}y)\leq c_{tot}(x)\circ_{i}c_{tot}(y)
.
∎

Example 4.12
.

x
and
y
such that
c_{tot}(x\circ_{i}y)<c_{tot}(x)\circ_{i}c_{tot}(y)
. For
x=(121|1)
and
y=(1|12|2)
, one has
(121|1)\circ_{1}(1|12|2)=(1312|2)
and
