4.3
Action on cochains

One has the obvious relative version of
[
BB09
, Proposition 2.20]
:

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two simplicial sets equipped with a simplicial map
f:Y\to X
.
The pair
(\mathbb{Z}[X],\mathbb{Z}[Y])
(resp.
(\Hom(\mathbb{Z}[X],\mathbb{Z}),\Hom(\mathbb{Z}[Y],\mathbb{Z}))
) is a coalgebra (resp. an algebra) over
\mathcal{RL}
. In particular,
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
acts on
(C^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}),C^{*}(Y;\mathbb{Z}))
.

Proof.

The
\mathcal{RL}
-coaction in the closed case is as in
[
BB09
, Proposition 2.20]
.
Otherwise

\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};\underline{n})]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[Y_{n}]\to\text{tensor products of $\mathbb{Z}[X_{n_{i}}]$ and $\mathbb{Z}[Y_{n_{j}}]$},

is given by
x\otimes y\mapsto\text{tensor products of }x^{*}_{i}(y)
and
f(x^{*}_{j}(y))
, where the
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
are the components of the dual interpretation of
x
from Section
4.1

i.e.

x
corresponds to the
k
-tuple
(x_{1},...,x_{k})\in\triangle([e_{1}],[e])\times...\times\triangle([e_{k}],[e])
.
Since
f
is a map of simplicial sets the result directly follows from
loc. cit.

∎

4.4
Action on iterated relative loop spaces

In this section we show that, for any two topological spaces
Y\subset X
pointed at the same point
*
, the operads
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{Top})
and
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{Top})
acts on the pair
(\Omega^{m}X,\Omega^{m}(X,Y))
.
Here,
\Omega^{m}X
denotes the
m
-fold loop space of
X
and
\Omega^{m}(X,Y)
denotes the
m
-fold relative loop spaces of
(X,Y)
.

For
m\geq 1
let
\Delta^{m}
be the simplicial
m
-simplex and
\partial\Delta^{m}
its boundary.
For
0\leq p\leq m
, let
\Lambda_{p}^{m}
be the
m
-horn at
p
.
Explicitly,
(\Lambda_{p}^{m})_{k}\subset\Delta^{m}_{k}
is given by elements of the form
yz:[k]\to[m]
for
z:[k]\to[n]
and
y:[n]\to[m]
with
n<m
and, if
n=m-1
, then
p\in Im(y)
.
For more details on these simplicial sets we refer to
[
GJ09
, p.6-7]
.
It immediately follows from the definitions that:

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
x\in\Delta^{m}
. If
x\notin{\Lambda_{p}^{m}}
, then there is a
k\in\{m-1,m\}
such that
x=\iota\rho:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[k]\hookrightarrow[m]
. Moreover, if
k=m-1
, then
\iota=\partial_{p}:[m-1]\hookrightarrow[m]
is the
p
-face map (
p\notin Im(\partial_{p})
);
if
k=m
, then
\iota=id:[m]\to[m]
is the identity map.
In particular, if
x\notin\partial\Delta^{m}
, then
k=m
, hence
x
is surjective.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{m}=\Delta^{m}/\partial\Delta^{m}
be the simplicial
m
-sphere.
Let
K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m]:=(\mathbb{S}^{m},*)
and
K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m]:=(\Delta^{m}/{\Lambda_{p}^{m}},{\partial\Delta^{m}}/{\Lambda_{p}^{m}})
.
Both
K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m]
and
K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m]
are simplicial objects in the category
CFin
whose objects are pairs
(A,B)
of finite sets pointed at the same point with
*\subset B\subset A
and morphisms
f:(A,B)\to(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
are morphisms of pointed sets
f:A\to A^{\prime}
such that
f(B)\subset B^{\prime}
. The
wedge sum
is given by
(A,B)\vee(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=(A\vee A^{\prime},B\vee B^{\prime})
.

The inclusion
\Lambda_{p}^{m}\subset\partial\Delta^{m}
induces the projection

\pi:K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m]\to K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m]
that is a map of simplicial objects in
CFin
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
m\geq 1
and
0\leq p\leq m
.
If
p
is odd, then the pair
(K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m],K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m])
is a coalgebra over
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
in
CFin
.
If
p
is even, then the pair
(K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m],K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m])
is a coalgebra over
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
in
CFin
.

Proof.

Let us fix
m\geq 1
. In order to alleviate notations one will denote
K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m]
(resp.
K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m]
) by
K^{\mathfrak{cl}}
(resp.
K^{\mathfrak{op}}
).
An element
x\in\mathcal{RL}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
induces maps

x^{*}:K^{\mathfrak{cl}}_{n}\to K^{\mathfrak{cl}}_{e_{1}}\times\cdots\times K^{\mathfrak{cl}}_{e_{k}}\text{ if }e=n\text{ and }\hat{x}^{*}:K^{\mathfrak{op}}_{\underline{n}}\to K^{c_{1}}_{e_{1}}\times\cdots\times K^{c_{k}}_{e_{k}}\text{ if }e=\underline{n}.

For the closed part (
e=n
), this is
[
BB09
, Proposition 2.16]
.
For
e=\underline{n}
, let
(x_{1},...,x_{k})\in\triangle([e_{1}],[\underline{n}])\times\cdots\times\triangle([e_{k}],[\underline{n}])
be the
k
-tuple corresponding to
x
in the dual interpretation of
\mathcal{RL}
, see Section
4.1
.
For
1\leq i\leq k
, let
n_{i}=ev(e_{i})
.
For
y\in\Delta^{m}/{\Lambda_{p}^{m}}
, the
i
-th component of
\hat{x}^{*}(y)
is given by:

\hat{x}_{i}^{*}(y)=\begin{cases}x_{i}^{*}(y)&\text{ if }x_{i}:[\underline{n_{i}}]\to[\underline{n}]\\
\pi(x_{i}^{*}(y))&\text{ if }x_{i}:[{n_{i}}]\to[\underline{n}].\end{cases}

We prove that if
x\in\mathcal{RL}_{m}
for
p
odd (resp.
x\in\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
for
p
even) then
\hat{x}^{*}(y)
belongs to
K^{c_{1}}_{e_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee K^{c_{k}}_{e_{k}}
.
From now on, let us suppose that there are
i\neq j
such that
x_{i}^{*}(y)
and
x_{j}^{*}(y)
are not at the base point.

Suppose
i
corresponds to an open colour (
i.e.

e_{i}=\underline{n_{i}}
).
In this case,
x_{i}^{*}(y):[n_{i}]\to[n]\to[m]
is not at the base point
i.e.

x_{i}^{*}(y)\notin\Lambda_{p}^{m}
. From Lemma
4.14
, this means that

Im(x_{i})\cap y^{-1}(r)\neq\emptyset
for each
r\in[m]
such that
r\neq p
.

1.

Suppose
j
corresponds to an open colour.
This means that
x_{j}^{*}(y_{s})
is not at the base point and then
Im(x_{j})\cap y^{-1}(r)\neq\emptyset
for each
r\in[m]
such that
r\neq p
. Therefore,
\underline{i}
and
\underline{j}
appear in
m
common fibres of
y
. Consequently,
c_{i,j}(x)\geq m>m-1
.

2.

Suppose
j
corresponds to a closed colour. This means that
\pi(x_{j}^{*}(y))\notin\partial\Delta^{m}
, that is,
x_{j}^{*}(y):[n_{j}]\to[n]\to[m]
does not belong to
\partial\Delta^{m}
and thus is surjective (Lemma
4.14
). It follows that
Im(x_{j})
intersects all the fibres of
y

i.e.

Im(x_{j})\cap y^{-1}(r)\neq\emptyset
for each
{r\in[m]}
. In particular,
y:[n]\to[m]
is surjective and then there are two cases for
x_{i}
:

(a)

Im(x_{i})\cap y^{-1}(p)\neq\emptyset
(
i.e.

Im(x_{i})\cap y^{-1}(r)\neq\emptyset
for each
r\in[m]
) and then
c_{i,j}(x)\geq m+1>m
(because
\underline{i}
and
{j}
appear in
m+1
common fibres of
y
); or,

(b)

Im(x_{i})\cap y^{-1}(p)=\emptyset
.
When
p
is odd, this implies that
c_{{i},j}(x)>m
if
j^{-}<\underline{i}^{-}
and
c_{{i},j}(x)>m-1
if
j^{-}>\underline{i}^{-}
.
When
p
is even, this implies that
c_{{i},j}(x)>m
if
j^{-}>\underline{i}^{-}
and
c_{{i},j}(x)>m-1
if
j^{-}<\underline{i}^{-}
.

Let us illustrate what happens in 2(b) when
p
is odd and
j^{-}<\underline{i}^{-}
. In the following integer-string representation of
x
,

x=(\underbrace{\overbrace{j\underline{i}}^{0}|\cdots|\overbrace{\underline{i}j}^{p-2}|\overbrace{j\underline{i}}^{p-1}}_{c_{{i},j}(x)_{|0,p-1}\geq p}|\overbrace{j}^{p}|\underbrace{\overbrace{j\underline{i}}^{p+1}|\cdots|\overbrace{\underline{i}j}^{m-1}|\overbrace{j\underline{i}}^{m}}_{c_{{i},j}(x)_{|p+1,m}\geq m-p})\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{with }j^{-}<\underline{i}^{-},

we only represent (relevant occurrences of) the integers
\underline{i}
and
j
; the
m+1
substrings represent the fibres
y^{-1}(r)
,
r\in[m]
so that, if
n>m
, then these substrings would be subdivided (to end up with
n
vertical bars).
Condition 2 says that the
j
are present in each of the
m+1
substrings.
The case 2(b) says that the
\underline{i}
are present in all the
m+1
substrings except the substring
p
.
The number of switches
c_{{i},j}(x)_{|0,p-1}
(resp.
c_{{i},j}(x)_{|p+1,m}
) between
\underline{i}
and
j
in the first
p
substrings (resp. in the last
m-p
substrings) is then
\geq p
(resp.
\geq m-p
).
If
c_{{i},j}(x)_{|0,p-1}=p
, the conditions
j^{-}<\underline{i}^{-}
and
p
odd imply that, in the substring
p-1
, the occurrences of
j
all appear before that of
\underline{i}
. Therefore, because of the presence of
j
in the substring
p
, there is an additional switch
i.e.

c_{{i},j}(x)\geq c_{{i},j}(x)_{|0,p-1}+1+c_{{i},j}(x)_{|p+1,m}\geq p+1+m-p>m
.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

Let
*\subset Y\subset X
be topological spaces.
For
m\geq 1
, the pair
(\Omega^{m}X,\Omega^{m}(X,Y))
is an algebra over
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{Top})
and over
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{Top})
.

Proof.

By adjunction, the
m
-fold relative loop space
\Omega^{m}(X,Y)=Hom_{Top_{*}}(|K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m]|_{\delta_{Top}},(X,Y))
is homeomorphic to the
\delta_{Top}
-totalization of
(X,Y,*)^{(K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m],*)}
. Similarly, the
m
-fold loop space
\Omega^{m}X=Hom_{Top_{*}}(|K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m]|_{\delta_{Top}},X)
is homeomorphic to the
\delta_{Top}
-totalization of
(X,*)^{(K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m],*)}
.
Proposition
4.15
implies that
((X,*)^{(K_{\mathfrak{cl}}[m],*)},(X,Y,*)^{(K^{p}_{\mathfrak{op}}[m],*)})
is an
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
-algebra for
p
odd, and an
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
-algebra for
p
even. The result follows from condensation (Section
2.5
).
∎

5
The relative surjection operad

We define two SC type operads
\mathcal{RS}_{m}
and
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}
that are sub-operads of
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
and
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
, respectively.
We show that these inclusions are weak equivalences.

Since we are using
\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}
-realization, the Dold-Kan correspondence provides a convenient way to present the cosimplicial chain complex
\xi_{c_{1},...,c_{k};c}(\mathcal{RL}_{m})(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
as well as the operad
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
. We closely follows
[
BBM13
, Section 3]
in which this point of view is adopted. In particular, we refer to
[
BBM13
, Section 3.3]
for normalized totalizations of (co)simplicial abelian groups; we adopt same notations except that, since we have defined
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
in the category of sets, we apply
\mathbb{Z}[-]
to make it a multisimplicial/cosimplicial abelian group.
This way, one identifies

\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})

with
\overline{Nor}\underline{Nor}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}_{m}])
, where
\underline{Nor}(-)
stands for the normalized realization of multisimplicial abelian groups and
\overline{Nor}(-)
stands for the normalized totalization of cosimplicial dg-abelian groups.

Let
(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
be a
(k+1)
-tuple of colours in
\{\mathfrak{cl},\mathfrak{op}\}
.
As complexes, we set

\mathcal{RS}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)^{*}:=\underline{Nor}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}_{m}(-,...,-;e)]),

where
e
is of type
c
such that
ev(e)=0
and the colours in the
i
-th input are of type
c_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
More explicitly,
\mathcal{RS}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)^{*}
is obtained from

\bigoplus_{*=-(n_{1}+...+n_{k})}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)]
,
where
e
is of type
c
such that
ev(e)=0
and
n_{i}:=ev(e_{i})
and
e_{i}
is of type
c_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
,
by modding out the images of the simplicial degeneracies.

We closely follow
[
BBM13
, equation (4) and (5)]
in which the following
whiskering
map and partial compositions are defined for the closed case.

On an integer-string
x\in\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
with
ev(e)=0
, the
n
-whiskering
w_{n}(x)
is a signed sum of integer-strings obtained from
x
by:
copying integers and adding a vertical bar between each copy (e.g.
i\mapsto i|i|i
)
with the requirement that the total number of vertical bars is
n
. Note that there is no bar between two adjacent integers with different values (e.g.
i|j
is not allowed).

Example 5.1
.

(\underline{1}|\underline{1}2|2|2)^{\mathfrak{op}}
is a term of
w_{3}((\underline{1}2)^{\mathfrak{op}})
.

The whiskering

\displaystyle w:\mathcal{RS}_{m}\to\overline{Nor}\underline{Nor}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}_{m}])

is defined by
x\mapsto\Pi w_{n}(x)
for
x\in\mathcal{RL}_{m}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
with
ev(e)=0
, and is extended by linearity.

Let us describe the partial compositions of
{\mathcal{RS}}_{m}
.
For two integer-string
f\in{\mathcal{RS}}_{m}(c_{1},...,c_{k};c)
and
g\in{\mathcal{RS}}_{m}(d_{1},...,d_{j};c_{i})

we set

(5.1)

f\circ_{i}^{\mathcal{RS}_{m}}g=f\circ_{i}w_{val_{i}(f)}g,

where
val_{i}(f)
is one less than the number of occurrences of
\widetilde{c}_{i}
in the integer-string
f
. We extend the compositions
\circ_{i}^{\mathcal{RS}_{m}}
by linearity.

We define an operad
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}
in the same way as
\mathcal{RS}_{m}
, by replacing
\mathcal{RL}_{m}
by
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}
in the above paragraphs.

Proposition 5.2
.

The partial compositions
\circ_{i}^{\mathcal{RS}_{m}}
(resp.
\circ_{i}^{\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}}
) endow
\mathcal{RS}_{m}
(resp.
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}
) with an operad structure.
The inclusion
w:\mathcal{RS}_{m}\hookrightarrow\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
(resp.
w^{\prime}:\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{m}\hookrightarrow\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{m}}(\delta_{\mathbb{Z}})
) is a weak equivalence of operads.

Proof.

Except for signs, the fact that
w
is compatible with the operadic structures is straightforward from the definition.
To get signs for the whiskering, we can proceed by a laborious induction, by requiring the whiskering to be a morphism of operads.
One can also remark that, concerning signs, the type of the colours (closed or open) does not matter.
This is because closed and open colours obey the same (co)simplicial and composition rules (with different constraints).
Thus, signs can be chosen using the same method than in the non-relative case (disregarding -for signs only- the type of colours): one could proceed as proposed in
[
BBM13
, Proposition 3.2]
, that is, embed
\mathcal{RS}_{m}
into the operad of coendomorphisms of the chains of a high dimensional simplex and then, choose signs in such a way that they match with those of
[
BF04
, Section 2.2]
in the closed and in the open cases and, in fact, in the open/closed case, disregarding type of colours when choosing signs. This can be compared with Proposition
4.13
, by asking the map
f
to be
id:\Delta^{n}\to\Delta^{n}
.

Let us denote by

\pi:\overline{Nor}\underline{Nor}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}_{m}])\to\underline{Nor}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}_{m}])^{0}=\mathcal{RS}_{m}

the map induced by the projection
p:\underline{Nor}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}_{m}])^{\cdot}\to\underline{Nor}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{RL}_{m}])^{0}
.
Such a map is a weak equivalence by Proposition
4.10
. Moreover it satisfies
\pi\circ w=id
. Thus,
w
is a weak equivalence.
∎

6
The operads
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
and
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}

6.1
The operad
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
in term of trees

6.1.1
The sets of
\mathcal{RT}

In what follows one will consider
planar rooted trees
; one refers to
[
MSS02
, II, Section 1.5]
for the terminology.
Our trees have only one external edge, called the
root
; all the other edges have
2
adjacent vertices. The external vertices (vertices that are adjacent to only one edge) are called the
leaves
.
Given a vertex
v
of a rooted tree
T
, the minimal sub-tree of
T
containing both the root and
v
has only one edge originating from
v
; such an edge is called the
output of
v
. The edges originating from a vertex that are not the output are called the
inputs
.
In our planar trees, the set of the edges originating from a vertex
v
is cyclically ordered in the clockwise direction.
This induces a linear order on the set of the inputs of
v
. This also canonically endows the set of the leaves with a linear order.

One considers planar rooted trees with
4
types of vertices:
white round-shaped

,
called
closed vertices
;
white square-shaped

,
called
open vertices
;
black round-shaped

, called
neutral vertices
;
and, black arrow-shaped

, called the
arrows
.
A
white
vertex is either a closed vertex or an open vertex.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
T
be a planar rooted tree.
Let
\nu
be a vertex of
T
. We denote by
T_{\nu}
the maximal sub-tree of
T
such that the output of
\nu
is the root of
T_{\nu}
.

Let
(e_{1},...,e_{k},e)
be a
k+1
-tuple of colours in
Col
, the set

\mathcal{RT}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
is the empty set if
e\in Col_{\mathfrak{cl}}
and there exists a
i
such that
e_{i}\in Col_{\mathfrak{op}}
;
otherwise,
\mathcal{RT}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)

is the set of equivalence classes of planar rooted trees
T
, satisfying the following:

•

the set of the arrows is a subset of the leaves of
T
and is of cardinal
ev(e)
.

•

the set of the closed (resp. open) vertices is labelled by the set
\{i\in\{1,...,k\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e_{i}=n_{i}\}
(resp.
\{i\in\{1,...,k\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e_{i}=\underline{n}_{i}\}
),
in such a way that:

(F1)

the vertex labelled by
i\in\{1,...,k\}
has
ev(e_{i})
inputs,

(F2)

there is no white vertex above an open vertex
i.e.
if

\nu
is an open vertex,
then
in the tree
T_{\nu}
the vertex
\nu
is the unique white vertex,

•

if
e
is an open colour (
i.e.

e=\underline{n}
for some
n
), then the root of
T
is decorated by an
\mathfrak{op}
.

The equivalence class is the same as in
[
DTT11
, 3.2.1]
. Explicitly, it is the finest one in which two planar rooted trees are equivalent if one of them can be obtained from the other by either:

- the contraction of an edge with neutral adjacent vertices; or,

- removing an neutral vertex with only one edge originating from it and joining the two edges adjacent to this vertex into one edge.

Remark 6.2
.

In the closed case, the trees of
\mathcal{RT}(n_{1},...,n_{k};n)
have no open vertices.

\mathcal{RT}(3,2,2;6)
.

An element of

.

(c)

An element of
\mathcal{RT}(2,2;\underline{5})
.

Examples of trees in
\mathcal{RT}
.

The operadic structure of
\mathcal{RT}
is explicitly given in the next section and corresponds to the substitution of trees into white vertices.

6.1.2
Correspondence between
\mathcal{RT}
and
\mathcal{RL}_{2}

Let us start by constructing a bijection of sets
\Phi:\mathcal{RT}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)\to\mathcal{RL}_{2}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
for each
k+1
-tuple
(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
of colours.

•

The map
\Phi
.
For a
T\in\mathcal{RT}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
let us construct an integer-string in
\mathcal{RL}_{2}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
as follows.
One runs through the tree
T
in clockwise direction starting from the root in such a way that one passes exactly two times on each edges (once per direction).
On our way, each time one meets a closed (resp. an open) vertex labelled by an
i\in\{1,...,k\}
one writes down the corresponding label
i
(resp. the corresponding label with an underline
\underline{i}
) and each time one meets an arrow one writes down a vertical bar. One adds an extra label
\mathfrak{op}
if the root is decorated by
\mathfrak{op}
.

•

The map
\Phi^{-1}
. To an integer-string representation one assigns a tree with: one closed (resp. open) vertex for each different integer (resp. underlined integer) and one arrow for each vertical bar;
the white vertices have one input less than the number of occurrences for the corresponding integer;
the corresponding tree is constructed such that its order fits with the reading (from the left to the right) of the integer-string.
One adds an extra label
\mathfrak{op}
on the root if the integer-string is decorated by
\mathfrak{op}
.
Note that when two equal integers (or two vertical bars) are adjacent in the integer-string this forces the creation of a neutral vertex.

Example 6.3
.

The tree from Figure
1(b)
corresponds to the integer-string
(1|1|13||3\underline{2}3\underline{4}|\underline{4}|\underline{4}\underline{4}3)^{\mathfrak{op}}
.

We endow
\mathcal{RT}
with an operadic structure by transferring the composition maps of
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
along the bijections above. Explicitly,
T\circ_{v}T^{\prime}:=\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(T)\circ_{v}\Phi(T^{\prime}))
for all composable
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{RT}
.
One can check that the composition maps in
\mathcal{RT}
are given by substitution of planar rooted trees into white vertices:
let
T\in\mathcal{RT}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
and let
v
be a vertex of
T
labelled by
e_{i}
. For a tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{RT}(f_{1},...,f_{j};e_{i})
, the tree
T\circ_{v}T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{RT}(e_{1},...,e_{i-1},f_{1},...,f_{j},e_{i+1},...,e_{k};e)
is given as follows. The vertex
v
of
T
is substituted by the tree
T^{\prime}
in such a way that:

•

the root of
T^{\prime}
is identified with the root of
v
;

•

the ordered set of the
n_{i}=ev(e_{i})
arrows of
T^{\prime}
is identified with the ordered set of the
n_{i}
inputs of
v
.

The set of the white vertices of
T\circ_{v}T^{\prime}
is labelled by the set
\{1,...,{k+j-1}\}
associated to
(e_{1},...,e_{i-1},f_{1},...,f_{j},e_{i+1},...,e_{k})
.

\bullet
1
2
{3}
\mathfrak{op}
1
\bullet
1
2
=
\bullet
\bullet
1
3
{4}
2
\mathfrak{op}
=
\bullet
1
3
{4}
2
\mathfrak{op}

Figure 6.2:
An example of composition in
\mathcal{RT}
.

By construction one has:

Proposition 6.4
.

The coloured operads
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
and
\mathcal{RT}
are isomorphic.

6.2
The operad
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
in term of trees

This section is the analogue to the previous section for
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
.
We define an operad
\mathcal{RT}^{\prime}
as follows.
For a
(k+1)
-tuple
(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
of colours in
Col
,
the set
\mathcal{RT}^{\prime}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
is defined as
\mathcal{RT}(e_{1},...,e_{k};e)
is, except that the condition (F2) from Section
6.1.1
is replaced by the condition:

(F2’)

there is no white vertex below an open vertex

i.e.
if
\nu
is a white vertex of
T
, then in the tree
T_{\nu}
either the vertex
\nu
is the unique open vertex or there is no open vertex.

In particular, for the closed part,
\mathcal{RT}^{\prime}(n_{1},...,n_{k};{n})=\mathcal{RT}(n_{1},...,n_{k};{n})
.

The operadic composition for
\mathcal{RT}^{\prime}
is as for
\mathcal{RT}
and we have the analogue of Proposition
6.4
:

Proposition 6.5
.

The coloured operads
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
and
\mathcal{RT}^{\prime}
are isomorphic.

Here is an example of an element of
\mathcal{RT}^{\prime}
and its corresponding element in
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
:

\mathcal{RT}^{\prime}(3,2,\underline{2};\underline{6})\ni\hbox to76.8pt{\vbox to64.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 30.36pt\lower-18.47905pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

6.3
A few remarks on the operads
\mathcal{RS}_{2}
and
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{2}

Proposition 6.6
.

As an operad,
\mathcal{RS}_{2}
is generated by the following elements

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathfrak{cl}}

\displaystyle=(12),\penalty\ \penalty\ T_{k}=(1213\cdots 1k1)\text{ for }k\geq 2,\text{ (closed part)}

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathfrak{op}}

\displaystyle=(\underline{1}\underline{2})^{\mathfrak{op}},\text{ (open part)}

\displaystyle T_{\underline{j}}

\displaystyle=(1\underline{2}1\underline{3}\cdots 1\underline{j}1)^{\mathfrak{op}}\text{ for }j\geq 2,\penalty\ \penalty\ inc=(1)^{\mathfrak{op}},

and the two unit elements
id_{\mathfrak{cl}}=(1)
and
id_{\mathfrak{op}}=(\underline{1})^{\mathfrak{op}}
. As an operad,
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{2}
is generated by

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathfrak{cl}},\penalty\ \penalty\ T_{k}\text{ for }k\geq 2,\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu_{\mathfrak{op}},\penalty\ \penalty\ T^{\prime}_{\underline{j}}=(\underline{1}2\underline{1}3\cdots\underline{1}j\underline{1})^{\mathfrak{op}}\text{ for }j\geq 2,\penalty\ \penalty\ inc,\penalty\ \penalty\ id_{\mathfrak{cl}}\text{ and }id_{\mathfrak{op}}.

Proof.

We prove the statement for
\mathcal{RS}_{2}
, the case
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{2}
is similar.
We suppose by induction on
N
that any integer-string of
\mathcal{RS}_{2}
with
N
different integers is obtained by operadic compositions of elements cited in the statement. The cases
N=1
and
N=2
are trivially verified.

In what follows we abusively do not mark the distinction between underlined and non underlined integers.
Let
x
be an integer-string of
\mathcal{RS}_{2}
with
N+1
different integers.
Because of the filtration condition (
4.2
),
x
can be written as a sequence
(A_{1}\cdots A_{n})
where the
A_{i}
’s are non empty sequences of integers such that, if
j
belongs to
A_{i}
, then
j\notin A_{s}
for
s\neq i
. Moreover, because of the symmetric group action, one can suppose that the integers of
A_{i}
are smaller than the integers of
A_{j}
whenever
i<j
. In this case, if
n>1
, then
x=(\alpha\circ_{1}(A_{1}\cdots A_{n-1}))\circ_{\max A_{n-1}+1}(\widetilde{A}_{n})
where
\widetilde{A}_{n}
is obtained from
A_{n}
by decreasing each number by
\max A_{n-1}
and
\alpha
is
(12),(\underline{1}\underline{2})^{\mathfrak{op}},(\underline{1}2)^{\mathfrak{op}}
,
(1\underline{2})^{\mathfrak{op}}
or
(12)^{\mathfrak{op}}
.
Since the
A_{i}
’s are not empty,
(A_{1}\cdots A_{n-1})
as well as
A_{n}
have at most
N
different integers and thus satisfy the induction hypotheses.
If
n=1
then either
x
is
T_{k}
(or
T_{\underline{k}}
) for some
k
or,
x
is such that

A_{1}=jB_{1}jB_{2}j\cdots jB_{p}j
with
1\leq p<N
and for some integer
j
. Thus there exists at least one
B_{i_{0}}
that contains
2\leq q\leq N-(p-1)
elements and
x=(jB_{1}j\cdots B_{i_{0}-1}jajB_{i_{0}+1}j\cdots jB_{p}j)\circ_{a}\widetilde{B}_{i_{0}}
for some
a
, which concludes the proof.
∎

, the generators of
\mathcal{RS}_{2}
described in Proposition
6.6
above, corresponds to the trees:

The operadic structure of
\mathcal{RS}_{2}
is described in terms of trees by means of Proposition
6.4
;
the whiskering
w
defined in Section
5
has a corresponding map on
\mathcal{RT}
(roughly, it consists in adding arrows linked to the white vertices) and the composition of two trees is given by transferring the formula (
5.1
) to
\mathcal{RT}
.
One has a similar description for
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{2}
.
For the closed part, detailed are given in
[
BBM13
]
with a slightly different convention.
A full description of the operad
\mathcal{RS}^{\prime}_{2}
in terms of similar trees is given in
[
HLQ16
]
.

6.4
The algebras over
\mathcal{RL}_{2}

We describe the algebras over
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
where, implicitly,
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
is seen in
C
by means of the strong symmetric monoidal functor
\textbf{Set}\to\textbf{C}
,
E\mapsto\coprod_{e\in E}1_{\textbf{C}}
.
Precisely, we show that
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
encodes the pairs
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})
subject to the following conditions.

I.

\mathcal{M}
is a multiplicative non-
\Sigma
operad.

II.

\mathcal{Z}\in\textbf{BiMod}_{\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{A}s}
and there is a morphism
\iota:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{Z}
in
\textbf{BiMod}_{\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{A}s}
.

Let
E
be the category with objects the pairs
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})
satisfying the two conditions
I
and
II
above; morphisms are the pairs

(f,g):(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})\to(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
subject to the following conditions:

•

f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a morphism of multiplicative non-
\Sigma
operads;

•

g:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is an
f
-equivariant morphism of left modules over
\mathcal{M}
;

•

g
is a morphism of bimodules over
\mathcal{A}s
; and,

•

\iota^{\prime}\circ f=g\circ\iota
.

Remark 6.7
.

Note that
E
is well defined since, by the last condition
\iota^{\prime}\circ f=g\circ\iota
, the morphism
\iota
of
II
associated to
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})
is unique.
Remark that the pair
(\mathcal{A}s,\mathcal{A}s)
is the initial object of
E
.

Remark 6.8
.

As observed in Example
2.6
, since here we have a morphism
\mathcal{A}s\to\mathcal{Z}
, it follows from
II
that
\mathcal{Z}
is, in particular, a weak bimodule over
\mathcal{A}s
(see also proof of Proposition
6.9
). By Lemma
2.7
this is equivalent to say that
\mathcal{Z}
is endowed with a cosimplicial structure. Likewise, the multiplicative structure of
\mathcal{M}
endows it with a cosimplicial structure.
These are the cosimplicial structures involved in the
\delta
-totalization that gives rise to the
\Coend_{\mathcal{RL}_{2}}(\delta)
-algebra
(Tot_{\delta}\mathcal{M},Tot_{\delta}\mathcal{Z})
.

Proposition 6.9
.

The category of
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
-algebras in
C
is isomorphic to the category
E
.

Proof.

We use the interpretation of
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
in terms of planar trees, see Proposition
6.4
.

Let us start with an
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
-algebra
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})
. This means that for each
T\in\mathcal{RL}_{2}(e_{1},...,e_{p};e)
one has a corresponding operation:

T\in\Hom(\textit{products of $\mathcal{M}(n_{i})$ and $\mathcal{Z}(n_{j})$},\mathcal{Z}(n))\text{ or, }T\in\Hom(\textit{products of $\mathcal{M}(n_{i})$},\mathcal{M}(n)),

according to the type of the output
e
, where
n_{s}=ev(e_{s})
is the value of
e_{s}
,
1\leq s\leq p
and
n=ev(e)
.

Let us write explicitly the induced structures on
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{Z}
.

1.

\mathcal{M}
: the partial compositions on elementary trees are given by

The operadic structure of
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
provides the associativity condition for partial compositions of
\mathcal{M}
. This is similar to (3) below, which we detail.

2.

The multiplicative structure on
\mathcal{M}
: the morphism
\alpha:\mathcal{A}s(k)\to\mathcal{M}(k)
is given by the corolla in a neutral vertex with
k
inputs. For
k=1
, one has
\alpha=\eta:1_{\textbf{C}}\to\mathcal{M}(1)
is the unit. Note that the isomorphism
\mathcal{A}s(k)\otimes\mathcal{A}s(l)\to\mathcal{A}s(k+l-1)
corresponds to the equivalence relation made on the neutral vertices (see Definition
6.1
).
The multiplication in
\mathcal{M}
is given by operations as:

i,k,l,x
are as in Definition
2.1
Item 2 and
\sigma_{(2,i),i+1}
is the block permutation (
\sigma_{(2,i),i+1}(s)=s+1
for
2\leq s\leq i
;
\sigma_{(2,i),i+1}(i+1)=2
and
\sigma_{(2,i),i+1}(s)=s
otherwise).
The first decomposition corresponds to the top-right path in the diagram of Definition
2.1
Item 2; the second decomposition corresponds to the left-bottom path.

Note that, by pre-composing
\lambda^{\mathcal{M}}
with
\alpha:\mathcal{A}s\to\mathcal{M}
, one has a left action of
\mathcal{A}s

The associativity for the right action is given by operadic composition of
\mathcal{RL}_{2}
. Precisely, for the left-sided square of Definition
2.1
Item 4, the left-bottom path results from
\mathcal{A}s(k)\otimes\mathcal{A}s(l)\cong\mathcal{A}s(k+l-1)
while the top-right path corresponds to the decomposition of the above tree as

6.

The morphism
\iota:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{Z}
is given by corollas in a closed vertex with an open output:

with a structure of weak bimodule over
\mathcal{A}s
.

,
the morphism
\mathcal{A}s\to\mathcal{Z}
given by the left-sided tree corresponds to
\iota\alpha:\mathcal{A}s\to\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{Z}
. More generally, note that any tree can be obtained as a composition of
elementary trees
: trees as in (1)-(4), (6) above and corollas in a neutral vertex.
Therefore, for
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})\in\textbf{E}
, the operation corresponding to a given a tree
T
is defined as given by any of the decompositions of
T
in elementary trees; the independence of this operation regarding the different decompositions is ensured by the properties of
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

corresponds to (
2
iterations of) the diagram 2. of Definition
2.1
, plus the fact that
\iota
is a left module map and
\eta:1_{\textbf{C}}\to\mathcal{M}
is the unit. Here, the penultimate tree is not elementary but it admits a decomposition into elementary trees as in (
6.1
).
∎

6.5
The algebras over
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}

We show that the operad
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
encodes the pairs
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})
subject to the following conditions.

I’.

\mathcal{M}
is a multiplicative non-
\Sigma
operad.

II’.

\mathcal{Z}\in\textbf{BiMod}_{\mathcal{A}s-\mathcal{M}}
with a morphism
\iota:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{Z}
in
\textbf{BiMod}_{\mathcal{A}s-\mathcal{M}}
.

Let
E’
be the category with objects the pairs
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})
satisfying the two conditions
I
’ and
II
’ above; morphisms are the pairs

(f,g):(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Z})\to(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
subject to the following conditions:

•

f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a morphism of multiplicative non-
\Sigma
operads;

•

g:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is an
f
-equivariant morphism of right modules over
\mathcal{M}
;

•

g
is a morphism of bimodules over
\mathcal{A}s
; and,

•

\iota^{\prime}\circ f=g\circ\iota
.

Proposition 6.10
.

The category of
\mathcal{RL}^{\prime}_{2}
-algebras in
C
is isomorphic to the category
E’
.

Proof.

The only significant differences with Proposition
6.9
are the following.
The right action of
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{Z}
is given by the
k
-corollas in an open vertex with a closed vertex at an input:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\leftrightarrow\lambda_{b_{1},...,b_{j}}:\mathcal{A}s(k)\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Z}(m_{j})\to\mathcal{Z}(k-j+\sum_{1\leq p\leq j}m_{p}),

are obtained via the left action of
\mathcal{A}s
on
\mathcal{Z}
, using the fact that
\iota\alpha:\mathcal{A}s\to\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{Z}
is a morphism of left-modules.
∎
